Based modules over the ı \imath quantum group of type AI
Hideya Watanabe

Abstract
. This paper studies classical weight modules over the ı \imath quantum group 𝐔 ı \mathbf{U}^{\imath} of type AI. We introduce the notion of based 𝐔 ı \mathbf{U}^{\imath} -modules by generalizing the notion of based modules over quantum groups (quantized enveloping algebras). We prove that each finite-dimensional irreducible classical weight 𝐔 ı \mathbf{U}^{\imath} -module with integer highest weight is a based 𝐔 ı \mathbf{U}^{\imath} -module. As a byproduct, a new combinatorial formula for the branching rule from 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 n \mathfrak{sl}_{n} to 𝔰 ​ 𝔬 n \mathfrak{so}_{n} is obtained.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B37; Secondary 17B10

1.
Introduction

Based modules over quantum groups

In representation theory of quantum groups (quantized enveloping algebras), there is a notion of based modules. Let
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
denote the quantum group over the field
\mathbf{K}:=\mathbb{C}(q)
of rational functions in one variable
q
associated with a complex semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. A based
\mathbf{U}
-module is a
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with two distinguished structures; a crystal basis and a canonical basis, which is also known as a global crystal basis.

Crystal basis theory, which was discovered by Kashiwara
[
16
]
and Lusztig
[
22
]
, is a powerful tool in combinatorial representation theory. The crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
of a based
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is a local basis in the sense that it forms a basis of
M
at
q=\infty
(in the literature, the variable
q
in this paper corresponds to
q^{-1}
). To be more specific,
\mathcal{B}_{M}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{M}
is a free submodule of
M
over the ring
\mathbf{K}_{\infty}:=\mathbb{C}[q^{-1}]_{(q^{-1})}
of rational functions regular at
q=\infty
, called the crystal lattice. Although
\mathcal{B}_{M}
is not a genuine
\mathbf{K}
-basis of
M
, it has a lot of information about the
\mathbf{U}
-module structure of
M
.

Canonical basis theory was initiated by Lusztig
[
22
]
, and then Kashiwara
[
17
]
found a different approach. The canonical basis of a based
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is a globalization of the crystal basis in the sense that it tends to the crystal basis as
q
goes to
\infty
.

One of the most distinguished properties of based
\mathbf{U}
-modules is cellularity (
[
23
, Chapter 27]
), which gives a canonical stratification of based
\mathbf{U}
-modules.
This generalizes a well-known property of the Hecke algebra of type
A
with the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis
[
18
]
.

\imath
Quantum groups

Recall that the quantum group
\mathbf{U}
is a
q
-deformation of the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Namely,
\mathbf{U}
tends to
U(\mathfrak{g})
as
q
goes to
1
. There is another family of
q
-deformations of the universal enveloping algebras of complex Lie algebras, called the
\imath
quantum groups.

The
\imath
quantum groups appear in the theory of quantum symmetric pairs initiated by Letzter
[
21
]
unifying earlier constructions by Koornwinder
[
20
]
, Gavrilik and Klimyk
[
11
]
, Noumi
[
25
]
, and others. Kolb
[
19
]
generalized Letzter’s theory to symmetrizable Kac-Moody algebras. In the theory of quantum symmetric pairs, we consider a complex semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and an involutive Lie algebra automorphism
\theta
on
\mathfrak{g}
. Set
\mathfrak{k}:=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\theta(x)=x\}
to be the fixed point subalgebra. A quantum symmetric pair associated with
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})
is a pair
(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}^{\imath})
consisting of the quantum group
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
and its coideal subalgebra
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
which tends to
U(\mathfrak{k})
as
q
goes to
1
.
The
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
itself is referred to as an
\imath
quantum group.

The
\imath
quantum groups
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
(with the embeddings
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}
) are thought of as generalizations of the quantum groups in the sense that a quantum group
\mathbf{U}
(with the comultiplication map
\Delta:\mathbf{U}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}\simeq U_{q}(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g})
) is an
\imath
quantum group associated with
(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})
. Such an
\imath
quantum group is said to be of diagonal type.

Based on this viewpoint, many results in the theory of quantum groups have been generalized to the
\imath
quantum groups setting, e.g., the bar-involution
[
2
,
5
]
, the quasi-
K
-matrix
[
4
,
5
]
, the universal
K
-matrix
[
1
,
3
,
8
,
26
]
, quantum Schur and Howe dualities
[
4
,
10
,
27
]
, and the
\imath
canonical basis
[
4
,
5
]
.
However, the theory of based
\mathbf{U}
-modules has not been generalized to the
\imath
quantum groups setting.

Results

The aim of this paper is to define the notion of based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules, and study their properties for the
\imath
quantum group of type AI, i.e., the
\imath
quantum group associated with
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{k})\simeq(\mathfrak{sl}_{n},\mathfrak{so}_{n})
(see
[
29
,
30
]
for related works for the
\imath
quantum group of quasi-split type AIII).
The symmetric pair
(\mathfrak{sl}_{n},\mathfrak{so}_{n})
is of split type, and hence, its structure, such as defining relations, is relatively simple.
Moreover, it is one of the generalized Onsager algebras, which have background in integrable systems (see
[
28
]
and references therein for detail).

In
[
31
]
, the notion of classical weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules was introduced, and the finite-dimensional irreducible classical weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules were classified in terms of highest weight theory for several types including AI. The isoclasses of such irreducible modules are parametrized by the set
X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
of dominant integral weights for
\mathfrak{k}
. For each
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
, let
V(\nu)
denote the corresponding irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module.
Recall that the integral weights for
\mathfrak{k}\simeq\mathfrak{so}_{n}
are divided into two groups, integer weights and half integer weights.
Let
X_{\mathfrak{k},\text{int}}
denote the set of integer weights, and set
X_{\mathfrak{k},\text{int}}^{+}:=X^{+}\cap X_{\mathfrak{k},\text{int}}
.
The first main result in this paper is the following.

Theorem A

(Theorem
8.1.2
)
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
be an integer weight. Then,
V(\nu)
has a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure.

As a byproduct, we obtain a purely combinatorial formula for the branching rule from
\mathbf{U}
to
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
.
Before stating the formula, let us prepare some notation.
First of all, let us identify
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
with
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, where
m
denotes the rank of
\mathfrak{k}(\simeq\mathfrak{so}_{n})
:

m=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}\quad&\text{ if }\text{$n$ is even},\\
\frac{n-1}{2}\quad&\text{ if }\text{$n$ is odd}.\end{cases}

Let
\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i}
denote the Kashiwara operators acting on crystal bases
[
16
]
. Given a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module and its crystal basis
\mathcal{B}
, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}
, set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varphi_{i}(b):=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{F}_{i}^{k}b\neq 0\},\\
&\varepsilon_{i}(b):=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{E}_{i}^{k}b\neq 0\},\\
&\deg_{i}(b):=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{i}(b)\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{i}(b)\text{ is even},\\
\varepsilon_{i}(b)+1\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{i}(b)\text{ is odd},\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{B}_{i}(b):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{E}_{i}b\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{i}(b)\text{ is even},\\
\widetilde{F}_{i}b\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{i}(b)\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Theorem B

(Theorem
8.2.6
)
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
and
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{3},\ldots,\nu_{2m-1})\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}=\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Let
[\lambda:\nu]
denote the multiplicity of the irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(\nu)
of highest weight
\nu
in the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
of highest weight
\lambda
.

(1)

Suppose
n
is even and
\nu_{2m-1}\neq 0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\lambda:\nu]=\frac{1}{2}\sharp\{b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\mid&\deg_{2i-1}(b)=|\nu_{2i-1}|\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\\
&\deg_{2i}(b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\\
&\deg_{2i+1}((\widetilde{B}_{2i}\widetilde{B}_{2i-1})^{|\nu_{2i+1}|}b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}\}.\end{split}

(2)

Suppose
n
is even and
\nu_{2m-1}=0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\lambda:\nu]=\sharp\{b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\mid&\deg_{2i-1}(b)=\nu_{2i-1}\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\\
&\deg_{2i}(b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\},\\
&\deg_{2i+1}((\widetilde{B}_{2i}\widetilde{B}_{2i-1})^{\nu_{2i+1}}b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}\}.\end{split}

(3)

Suppose
n
is odd. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}[\lambda:\nu]=\sharp\{b\in\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\mid&\deg_{2i-1}(b)=\nu_{2i-1}\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\\
&\deg_{2i}(b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\\
&\deg_{2i+1}((\widetilde{B}_{2i}\widetilde{B}_{2i-1})^{\nu_{2i+1}}b)=0\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}\}.\end{split}

Although
\mathfrak{k}
is not set-theoretically the same as
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
, the Lie algebra of
n
by
n
skew-symmetric matrices, this formula also tells us the branching rule from
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
to
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
by a similar argument to
[
24
, 2.4]
. This classical branching problem has been studied for a long time by many people, and several (partial) answers have been provided. Among them, Jang and Kwon
[
14
]
recently described this branching rule in terms of Littlewood-Richardson coefficients. Their formula identifies the multiplicities with the numbers of certain combinatorial objects; Littlewood-Richardson tableaux satisfying certain conditions. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
is realized as the set of semistandard tableaux of shape
\lambda
, our formula identifies the multiplicities with the numbers of certain semistandard tableaux. Hence, it would be interesting to construct an explicit bijection between such Littlewood-Richardson tableaux and semistandard tableaux.

Recall that the crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
of a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
tells us how
M
decomposes into irreducible ones. In particular, the crystal basis theory provides us a branching rule from
\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U}
to
\mathbf{U}
. Recall also that
(\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U})
is an
\imath
quantum group of diagonal type. Then, our formula can be seen as a generalization of the branching rule for the
\imath
quantum groups of diagonal type to that of type AI.

Organization

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we briefly review the theory of based modules over the quantum groups. In Section
3
, we formulate the notion of based modules over the
\imath
quantum groups by generalizing that of based modules over the quantum groups. From Section
4
on, we restrict our attention to the
\imath
quantum group of type AI. For such an
\imath
quantum group, we introduce the notion of standard
X^{\imath}
-weight modules, which plays a key role when proving that the finite-dimensional irreducible highest weight modules of integer highest weights are based modules. In Sections
5
–
7
, we analyze low-rank cases. Based on results obtained there, we finally prove our main theorems in Section
8
.

Notation

Throughout this paper, we use the following notation:

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
: the set of nonnegative integers.

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}
: the set of even integers.

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
: the set of odd integers.

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}}:=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}
.

•

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}:=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
.

•

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,
p(a):=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}\quad&\text{ if }a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\mathrm{odd}\quad&\text{ if }a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

•

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,
q(a):=p(a-1)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{odd}\quad&\text{ if }a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\mathrm{ev}\quad&\text{ if }a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

•

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
[a,b]:=\{c\in\mathbb{Z}\mid a\leq c\leq b\}
.

•

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p\in\{\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{odd}\}
,
[a,b]_{p}:=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•

\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\{\overline{0},\overline{1}\}
: the abelian group of order
2
.

•

\overline{\cdot}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
: the quotient map.

•

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
,
p(a):=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}\quad&\text{ if }a=\overline{0},\\
\mathrm{odd}\quad&\text{ if }a=\overline{1}.\end{cases}

2.
Quantum groups

In this section, we briefly review basic results concerning finite-dimensional based modules over a quantum group.
We refer the reader to
[
23
]
.

2.1.
Hermitian inner product

Let
\mathbf{K}
denote the field
\mathbb{C}(q)
of complex rational functions. In this subsection, we formulate the notion of
\mathbf{K}
-valued Hermitian inner product and generalize basic results about complex metric spaces.
The proofs of statements in this
subsection are straightforward, and hence, omitted.

Set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{K}_{1}:=\left\{\frac{f}{g}\in\mathbf{K}\mid f,g\in\mathbb{C}[q],\ g|_{q=1}\neq 0\right\},\\
&\mathbf{K}_{\infty}:=\left\{\frac{f}{g}\in\mathbf{K}\mid f,g\in\mathbb{C}[q^{-1}],\ g|_{q^{-1}=0}\neq 0\right\}.\end{split}

Let
\mathrm{ev}_{1}:\mathbf{K}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{1}/(q-1)\mathbf{K}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{C}
(resp.,
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathbf{K}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbf{K}_{\infty}/q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}\simeq\mathbb{C}
) denote the evaluation map at
q=1
(resp.,
q^{-1}=0
).

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
c\in\mathbf{K}^{\times}
, and write
c=\frac{f}{g}
with
f=\sum_{m}a_{m}q^{m}\in\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]
,
g\in 1+q^{-1}\mathbb{C}[q^{-1}]
.

(1)

The degree
\deg(c)\in\mathbb{Z}
of
c
is defined to be
\max\{m\mid a_{m}\neq 0\}
.

(2)

The leading coefficient
\operatorname{lc}(c)\in\mathbb{C}
of
c
is defined to be
a_{\deg(c)}
.

(3)

The leading term
\operatorname{lt}(c)\in\mathbb{C}q^{\mathbb{Z}}
of
c
is defined to be
\operatorname{lc}(c)q^{\deg(c)}
.

Furthermore, we set

\deg(0):=-\infty,\ \operatorname{lc}(0):=0,\ \operatorname{lt}(0):=0.

Let
z^{*}\in\mathbb{C}
denote the complex conjugate of
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Extend the notion of complex conjugate to a ring automorphism on
\mathbf{K}
by setting
q^{*}=q
.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbf{K}
-vector space. A (
\mathbf{K}
-valued) Hermitian inner product on
V
is a map
(\cdot,\cdot):V\times V\rightarrow\mathbf{K}
satisfying the following:

(1)

(au+bv,w)=a(u,w)+b(v,w)
for all
a,b\in\mathbf{K}
,
u,v,w\in V
.

(2)

(v,u)=(u,v)^{*}
for all
u,v\in V
.

(3)

\operatorname{lc}((v,v))\geq 0
for all
v\in V
.

(4)

\operatorname{lc}((v,v))=0
implies
v=0
.

(5)

\deg((v,v))\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}\sqcup\{-\infty\}
for all
v\in V
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product. For
v\in V
, we set

\deg(v):=\frac{1}{2}\deg((v,v)),\quad\operatorname{lc}(v):=\sqrt{\operatorname{lc}((v,v))},\quad\operatorname{lt}(v):=\operatorname{lc}(v)q^{\deg(v)}.

Here, we understand that
\frac{1}{2}(-\infty)=-\infty
and
q^{-\infty}=0
.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product.

(1)

v\in V
is said to be almost normal if
\operatorname{lt}(v)=1
.

(2)

u,v\in V
are said to be orthogonal (resp., almost orthogonal) if
(u,v)=0
(resp.,
(u,v)\in q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
).

(3)

A basis of
V
is said to be orthogonal if it consists of vectors which are pairwise orthogonal.

(4)

A basis of
V
is said to be almost orthonormal if it consists of almost normal vectors which are pairwise almost orthogonal.

Remark 2.1.5
.

Because of conditions (
3
)–(
5
) in Definition
2.1.2
, every nonzero vector can be normalized to an almost normal vector proportional to it.

The following is an easy analogue of an elementary result about complex metric spaces.

Proposition 2.1.6
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
, and
W\subseteq V
a subspace.

(1)

V
possesses an orthogonal and almost orthonormal basis.

(2)

The restriction
(\cdot,\cdot)_{W}:=(\cdot,\cdot)|_{W\times W}
is a Hermitian inner product on
W
.

(3)

Let
B_{W}
be an almost orthonormal basis of
W
. Then, there exists an almost orthonormal basis
B_{V}
of
V
which extends
B_{W}
.

Let
V
and
W
be as in Proposition
2.1.6
. In the sequel, unless otherwise stated, whenever we consider a Hermitian inner product of
W
, we assume that it is
(\cdot,\cdot)_{W}
in
2.1.6
(
2
).

For each
\mathbf{K}
-vector space
V
equipped with a Hermitian inner product, we use the following notation throughout this paper:

•

\mathcal{L}_{V}:=\{v\in V\mid(v,v)\in\mathbf{K}_{\infty}\}
.

•

\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V}:=\mathcal{L}_{V}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}
.

•

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{V}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
the quotient map.

Proposition 2.1.7
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
and an almost orthonormal basis
B
.

(1)

We have
\mathcal{L}_{V}=\mathbf{K}_{\infty}B
.

(2)

We have
\mathcal{L}_{V}=\{v\in V\mid(u,v)\in\mathbf{K}_{\infty}\text{ for all }u\in\mathcal{L}_{V}\}
.

(3)

(\cdot,\cdot)
induces a
\mathbb{C}
-valued Hermitian inner product (denoted by the same symbol) on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
given by

(\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(u),\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v)):=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((u,v)).

(4)

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B)
is an orthonormal basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V}
.

Proposition 2.1.8
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product. Suppose that
V
admits an orthogonal decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}V_{\omega}
. Then, we have orthogonal decompositions

\mathcal{L}_{V}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}\mathcal{L}_{V_{\omega}},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V}=\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V_{\omega}},

here, we identify
\mathcal{L}_{V_{\omega}}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}
with
\mathcal{L}_{V_{\omega}}/(q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}\cap\mathcal{L}_{V_{\omega}})
, which equals
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V_{\omega}}
.

Definition 2.1.9
.

Let
V,W
be
\mathbf{K}
-vector spaces equipped with Hermitian inner products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{V}
,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{W}
, respectively.

(1)

We say that a linear map
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{K}}(V,W)
almost preserves the metrics if for each
u,v\in\mathcal{L}_{V}
, we have
(f(u),f(v))_{W}-(u,v)_{V}\in q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
.

(2)

We say that
V
and
W
are almost isometric if there exists an isomorphism
V\rightarrow W
of vector spaces which almost preserves the metrics. Such an isomorphism is called an almost isometry.

The following is an easy analog of an elementary result about complex metric spaces.

Proposition 2.1.10
.

Let
V,W
be
\mathbf{K}
-vector spaces equipped with Hermitian inner products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{V}
,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{W}
, respectively. Let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{K}}(V,W)
. Then, the following are equivalent.

(1)

f
almost preserves the metrics.

(2)

For each almost orthonormal basis
B
of
V
, its image
f(B)
forms an almost orthonormal basis of
f(V)
.

(3)

There exists an almost orthonormal basis
B
of
V
such that
f(B)
forms an almost orthonormal basis of
f(V)
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product, and
W\subseteq V
a subspace. Throughout this paper, we always equip the quotient space
V/W
with a Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{V/W}
as follows. Let
B_{W}\subseteq B_{V}
be almost orthonormal bases of
W\subseteq V
. Set
W^{\prime}:=\mathbf{K}(B_{V}\setminus B_{W})
. For each
v\in V=W\oplus W^{\prime}
, let
v_{1}\in W
and
v_{2}\in W^{\prime}
be such that
v=v_{1}+v_{2}
. Also, let
[v]
denote the image of
v
in
V/W
. Then, define

([u],[v])_{V/W}:=(u_{2},v_{2}).

This is a Hermitian inner product on
V/W
. Clearly,
[B_{V}]:=\{[b]\mid b\in B_{V}\setminus B_{W}\}
forms an almost orthonormal basis of
V/W
. Then, by Proposition
2.1.7
(
1
), we have

\mathcal{L}_{V/W}=\mathbf{K}_{\infty}[B_{V}]=\mathbf{K}_{\infty}B_{V}/(W\cap\mathbf{K}_{\infty}B_{V})=\mathcal{L}_{V}/\mathcal{L}_{W}.

This implies that although the Hermitian inner product thus constructed depends on
B_{W}
and
B_{V}
, the
\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
-submodule
\mathcal{L}_{V/W}
does not.

2.2.
Quantum groups

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, set

[n]_{q^{a}}:=\frac{q^{an}-q^{-an}}{q^{a}-q^{-a}}.

Also, for
m\geq n\geq 0
, set

[n]_{q^{a}}!:=[n]_{q^{a}}\cdots[2]_{q^{a}}[1]_{q^{a}},\quad{m\brack n}_{q^{a}}:=\frac{[m]_{q^{a}}!}{[m-n]_{q^{a}}![n]_{q^{a}}!},

where we understand that
[0]_{q^{a}}!=1
. When
a=1
, we often omit the subscript
q^{a}
.

Let
A
be an associative algebra over
\mathbf{K}
. For
x,y\in A
,
z\in A^{\times}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

[z;n]_{q^{a}}:=\frac{q^{an}z-q^{-an}z^{-1}}{q^{a}-q^{-a}},\quad\{z;n\}_{q^{a}}:=q^{an}z+q^{-an}z^{-1},\quad[x,y]_{q^{b}}:=xy-q^{b}yx.

When
a=1
or
b=0
, we often omit the subscripts
q^{a}
or
q^{b}
, respectively.

Let
(a_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
be a Cartan matrix of finite type. We identify the index set
I
with the set of vertices of the Dynkin diagram of the Cartan matrix, or with the Dynkin diagram itself. Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
denote the set of simple roots,
\{h_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the set of simple coroots,
\{\varpi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the set of fundamental weights,
X:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{i}
the weight lattice,
Y:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}h_{i}
the coroot lattice,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:Y\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
the perfect pairing given by
\langle h_{i},\varpi_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
. Let
d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be such that
d_{i}a_{i,j}=d_{j}a_{j,i}
for all
i,j\in I
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}(I)
denote the finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
associated with the Dynkin diagram
I
. Let
e_{i},f_{i},h_{i}
,
i\in I
denote the Chevalley generators.

The quantum group
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is a unital associative algebra over
\mathbf{K}
generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{h}
,
i\in I
,
h\in Y
subject to the following relations; for each
i,j\in I
and
h,h^{\prime}\in Y
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&K_{0}=1,\quad K_{h}K_{h^{\prime}}=K_{h+h^{\prime}},\\
&K_{h}E_{i}=q^{\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}E_{i}K_{h},\quad K_{h}F_{i}=q^{-\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle}F_{i}K_{h},\\
&[E_{i},F_{j}]=\delta_{i,j}[K_{i};0]_{q_{i}},\\
&\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}{1-a_{i,j}\brack k}_{q_{i}}E_{i}^{1-a_{i,j}-k}E_{j}E_{i}^{k}=0\quad\text{ if }i\neq j,\\
&\sum_{k=0}^{1-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{k}{1-a_{i,j}\brack k}_{q_{i}}F_{i}^{1-a_{i,j}-k}F_{j}F_{i}^{k}=0\quad\text{ if }i\neq j,\end{split}

where
K_{i}:=K_{d_{i}h_{i}}
and
q_{i}:=q^{d_{i}}
.

The quantum group
\mathbf{U}
admits a Hopf algebra structure with comultiplication
\Delta
defined by

\Delta(E_{i}):=E_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}\otimes E_{i},\quad\Delta(F_{i}):=1\otimes F_{i}+F_{i}\otimes K_{i}^{-1},\quad\Delta(K_{h})=K_{h}\otimes K_{h}.

Let
\wp
denote the algebra anti-involution on
\mathbf{U}
defined by

\wp(E_{i}):=q_{i}^{-1}F_{i}K_{i},\quad\wp(F_{i}):=q_{i}^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},\quad\wp(K_{h}):=K_{h}.

By the definitions of
\Delta
and
\wp
, one can verify that

\Delta\circ\wp=(\wp\otimes\wp)\circ\Delta.

Let
(\cdot)^{*}:\mathbf{U}\rightarrow\mathbf{U}
denote the ring automorphism which extends the complex conjugate on
\mathbf{K}
in a way such that

E_{i}^{*}=E_{i},\ F_{i}^{*}=F_{i},\ K_{h}^{*}=K_{h},\text{ for all }i\in I,\ h\in Y.

Then, we have
\wp\circ*=*\circ\wp
, and
\Delta\circ*=(*\otimes*)\circ\Delta
. Therefore,
\wp^{*}:=\wp\circ*
is an algebra anti-involution, and satisfies

(1)

\displaystyle\Delta\circ\wp^{*}=(\wp^{*}\otimes\wp^{*})\circ\Delta.

Let
\operatorname{Br}=\operatorname{Br}(I)
denote the braid group associated with the Dynkin diagram
I
with generators
T_{i}
,
i\in I
. The braid group
\operatorname{Br}
acts on
\mathbf{U}
via Lusztig’s automorphisms
T^{\prime\prime}_{i,1}
,
i\in I
(
[
23
, 37.1.3]
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T_{i}(E_{j}):=\begin{cases}-F_{i}K_{i}\quad&\text{ if }j=i,\\
\sum_{r+s=-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{r}q_{i}^{-r}E_{i}^{(s)}E_{j}E_{i}^{(r)}\quad&\text{ if }j\neq i,\end{cases}\\
&T_{i}(F_{j}):=\begin{cases}-K_{i}^{-1}E_{i}\quad&\text{ if }j=i,\\
\sum_{r+s=-a_{i,j}}(-1)^{r}q_{i}^{r}F_{i}^{(r)}F_{j}F_{i}^{(s)}\quad&\text{ if }j\neq i,\end{cases}\\
&T_{i}K_{h}:=K_{h-\langle h,\alpha_{i}\rangle h_{i}},\end{split}

where
E_{i}^{(n)}:=\frac{1}{[n]_{q_{i}}!}E_{i}^{n}
and
F_{i}^{(n)}:=\frac{1}{[n]_{q_{i}}!}F_{i}^{n}
are divided powers.

2.3.
Weight
\mathbf{U}
-modules

Given a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
and a weight
\lambda\in X
, the subspace

M_{\lambda}:=\{m\in M\mid K_{h}m=q^{\langle h,\lambda\rangle}m\ \text{ for all }h\in Y\},

is called the weight space of
M
of weight
\lambda
, and each element of
M_{\lambda}
is said to be a weight vector of weight
\lambda
.
A
\mathbf{U}
-module is said to be a weight module if it admits a weight space decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda}
.

For each
\lambda,\mu\in X
, we write
\mu\leq\lambda
to indicate that
\lambda-\mu\in\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
. This defines a partial order on
X
, called the dominance order.

Let
X^{+}:=\{\lambda\in X\mid\langle h_{i},\lambda\rangle\geq 0\ \text{ for all }i\in I\}
denote the set of dominant weights.

Theorem 2.3.1

(see e.g.
[
23
, Part I]
)
.

(1)

For each weight
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
,
\lambda\in X
, and
i\in I
, we have

E_{i}M_{\lambda}\subseteq M_{\lambda+\alpha_{i}},\quad F_{i}M_{\lambda}\subseteq M_{\lambda-\alpha_{i}}.

(2)

Each finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module is completely reducible.

(3)

The isoclasses of finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-modules are parametrized by
X^{+}
. Let
V(\lambda)
denote the finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module of highest weight
\lambda\in X^{+}
, and
v_{\lambda}\in V(\lambda)
the highest weight vector.

(4)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, we have

V(\lambda)=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbf{K}}\{F_{i_{1}}\cdots F_{i_{r}}v_{\lambda}\mid r\geq 0,\ i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in I\}.

(5)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
and
\mu\in X
, we have
\dim V(\lambda)_{\lambda}=1
, and
\dim V(\lambda)_{\mu}=0
unless
\mu\leq\lambda
.

(6)

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
and
\mu\in X
such that
\mu<\lambda
, we have

V(\lambda)_{\mu}=\sum_{i\in I}F_{i}V(\lambda)_{\mu+\alpha_{i}}.

2.4.
\mathbf{U}
-modules with contragredient Hermitian inner products

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
. We say that
(\cdot,\cdot)
is contragredient if it satisfies

(2)

\displaystyle(xu,v)=(u,\wp^{*}(x)v)\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U},\ u,v\in M.

Proposition 2.4.2
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
, and consider the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
. Then, there exists a unique contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}
on
V(\lambda)
such that
(v_{\lambda},v_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{h}
,
i\in I
,
h\in Y
, and set
V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}v_{\lambda}
. Then, we have

\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathbf{K},\quad V(\lambda)=V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathbf{K}.

By
[
23
, Proposition 19.1.2]
, there exists a unique symmetric bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)^{\prime}_{\lambda}:V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}\times V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(q)
such that
(v_{\lambda},v_{\lambda})^{\prime}_{\lambda}=1
, and
(xu,v)^{\prime}_{\lambda}=(u,\wp(x)v)^{\prime}_{\lambda}
for all
x\in\mathbf{U}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
u,v\in V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Define a map
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}:V(\lambda)\times V(\lambda)\rightarrow\mathbf{K}
by

(au,bv)_{\lambda}:=ab^{*}(u,v)^{\prime}_{\lambda},\quad a,b\in\mathbf{K},\ u,v\in V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Then,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}
satisfies conditions (
1
) and (
2
) in Definition
2.1.2
, condition (
2
) in Definition
2.4.1
, and
(v_{\lambda},v_{\lambda})_{\lambda}=1
.

Moreover, by
[
23
, Lemma 19.1.4]
, there exists a basis
B
of
V(\lambda)_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that

(b,b^{\prime})^{\prime}_{\lambda}\in\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}+q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}.

This shows that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}
satisfies conditions (
3
)–(
5
) in Definition
2.1.2
. Thus the proof completes.
∎

Lemma 2.4.3
.

Let
M
and
N
be
\mathbf{U}
-modules with contragredient Hermitian inner products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{N}
. Then, the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M,N}
on
M\otimes N
given by

(m_{1}\otimes n_{1},m_{2}\otimes n_{2})_{M,N}:=(m_{1},m_{2})_{M}(n_{1},n_{2})_{N}

is a contragredient Hermitian inner product.

Proof.

The assertion follows from equation (
1
) on page
1
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.4
.

Let
M
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Then, we have
(M_{\lambda},M_{\mu})=0
for all
\lambda,\mu\in X
such that
\lambda\neq\mu
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from the fact that
\wp^{*}(K_{h})=K_{h}
for all
h\in Y
.
∎

Proposition 2.4.5
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Then, there exists an irreducible decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}M_{k}
satisfying the following:

(1)

(M_{k},M_{l})=0
unless
k=l
.

(2)

For each
k\in[1,r]
, there exists a unique
\lambda_{k}\in X^{+}
and an isomorphism
\phi_{k}:M_{k}\rightarrow V(\lambda_{k})
of
\mathbf{U}
-modules which is an almost isometry.

Proof.

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, set
H_{\lambda}:=\{m\in M_{\lambda}\mid E_{i}m=0\text{ for all }i\in I\}
. Then, by Proposition
2.1.6
(
1
),
H_{\lambda}
has an orthogonal basis. By Lemma
2.4.4
, we have
(H_{\lambda},H_{\mu})=0
if
\lambda\neq\mu
. Then one can take an orthogonal basis
m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}
of
\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X^{+}}H_{\lambda}
consisting of weight vectors. Setting
M_{k}:=\mathbf{U}m_{k}
, we obtain an irreducible decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}M_{k}
of
M
.

For each
k\in[1,r]
, let
\lambda_{k}\in X^{+}
be such that
m_{k}\in H_{\lambda_{k}}
. Then, we have
M_{k}\simeq V(\lambda_{k})
. Each element of
M_{k}
is of the form
xm_{k}
for some
x\in\mathbf{U}
. Let
k\neq l
and
x,y\in\mathbf{U}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\lambda_{k}\geq\lambda_{l}
. Then, we have

(xm_{k},ym_{l})=(m_{k},\wp^{*}(x)ym_{l}).

Since
\wp^{*}(x)ym_{l}\in M_{l}\simeq V(\lambda_{l})
, by Theorem
2.3.1
(
5
), it is a linear combination of weight vectors of weight less than or equal to
\lambda_{l}
, while
m_{k}
is of weight
\lambda_{k}(\geq\lambda_{l})
. Hence, if
\lambda_{l}\neq\lambda_{k}
, then we have
(m_{k},\wp^{*}(x)ym_{l})=0
by Lemma
2.4.4
. Otherwise, one can write as

\wp^{*}(x)ym_{l}=cm_{l}+m^{\prime},

where
c\in\mathbf{K}
, and
m^{\prime}
is a sum of weight vectors of weights less than
\lambda_{l}(=\lambda_{k})
. Then, we have

(m_{k},\wp^{*}(x)ym_{l})=c(m_{k},m_{l})=0

since
m_{k}
and
m_{l}
are orthogonal. Thus, we obtain that
(M_{k},M_{l})=0
, as desired.

For a proof of the second assertion, replace
m_{k}
with
\frac{1}{\operatorname{lt}(m_{k})}m_{k}
in the argument above. Then, we have
\operatorname{lt}(m_{k})=1
. Since
m_{k}
is a highest weight vector of weight
\lambda_{k}
, there exists an isomorphism
\phi_{k}:M_{k}\rightarrow V(\lambda_{k})
of
\mathbf{U}
-modules such that
\phi_{k}(m_{k})=v_{\lambda_{k}}
. Then, for each
u,v\in M_{k}
, we have

(u,v)=(m_{k},m_{k})(\phi_{k}(u),\phi_{k}(v))_{\lambda_{k}}.

In particular, for each
u,v\in\mathcal{L}_{M_{k}}
, we have

(\phi_{k}(u),\phi_{k}(v))_{\lambda_{k}}-(u,v)=((m_{k},m_{k})^{-1}-1)(u,v)\in q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}.

This implies that
\phi_{k}
is an almost isometry. Thus, the proof completes.
∎

2.5.
Crystal bases of
\mathbf{U}
-modules

Definition 2.5.1
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbf{K}
-vector space and
A
a subring of
\mathbf{K}
. An
A
-lattice of
V
, or a lattice of
V
over
A
, is a free
A
-submodule
L
of
V
such that
L\otimes_{A}\mathbf{K}=V
.

Example 2.5.2
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{K}
-vector space equipped with a Hermitian inner product. Then,
\mathcal{L}_{V}
is a
\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
-lattice of
V
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Let
\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i}
,
i\in I
denote the Kashiwara operators. They preserve
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, and hence, induce
\mathbb{C}
-linear operators on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
. A
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
satisfying certain conditions is called a crystal basis of
M
(see e.g.
[
16
]
for detail).
For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
and
i\in I
, set

\varphi_{i}(b):=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{F}_{i}^{k}b\neq 0\},\quad\varepsilon_{i}(b):=\max\{k\mid\widetilde{E}_{i}^{k}b\neq 0\}.

Let
M
and
N
be
\mathbf{U}
-modules with crystal bases
\mathcal{B}_{M}
and
\mathcal{B}_{N}
. Then,
\mathcal{B}_{M}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{N}
becomes a crystal basis of
M\otimes N
on which the Kashiwara operators and
\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i}
act as

(3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{F}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{F}_{i}(b_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})<\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\widetilde{F}_{i}(b_{1})\otimes b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\geq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{E}_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\begin{cases}b_{1}\otimes\widetilde{E}_{i}(b_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\leq\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\\
\widetilde{E}_{i}(b_{1})\otimes b_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})>\varphi_{i}(b_{2}),\end{cases}\\
&\varphi_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\varphi_{i}(b_{1})+\max(0,\varphi_{i}(b_{2})-\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})),\\
&\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1}\otimes b_{2})=\varepsilon_{i}(b_{2})+\max(0,\varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})-\varphi_{i}(b_{2})).\end{split}

These formulas can be found, for example, in
[
12
, Theorem 4.1]
; one should note that
\widetilde{F}_{i},\widetilde{E}_{i},\varphi_{i},\varepsilon_{i}
in this paper are
\widetilde{E}_{i},\widetilde{F}_{i},\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}
there.

When we consider the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
,
\lambda\in X^{+}
equipped with the contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda}
constructed in Proposition
2.4.2
, we set

\mathcal{L}(\lambda):=\mathcal{L}_{V(\lambda)},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda):=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V(\lambda)},\quad b_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v_{\lambda}).

By
[
17
, Theorem 2]
,
V(\lambda)
possesses a unique crystal basis
\mathcal{B}(\lambda)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda)
containing
b_{\lambda}
.

Now, let us turn back to a general
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
. By Proposition
2.4.5
, we have an orthogonal irreducible decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}M_{k}
. Then, by Proposition
2.1.8
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{M}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}\mathcal{L}_{k},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k},

where
\mathcal{L}_{k}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{k}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}:=\mathcal{L}_{k}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{k}
. Moreover, the almost isometries
\phi_{k}
in Proposition
2.4.5
induce isomorphisms

\mathcal{L}_{k}\simeq\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{k}),\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\simeq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda_{k}).

Let
\mathcal{B}_{k}
denote the basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
corresponding to
\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{k})
under the isomorphism
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\simeq\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda_{k})
above. Then, we obtain a crystal basis

\mathcal{B}_{M}:=\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{r}\mathcal{B}_{k}

of
M
. Furthermore, every crystal basis of
M
is of this form.

For each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, set
M[\lambda]:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{k}=\lambda\end{subarray}}M_{k}
to be the sum of all submodules of
M
isomorphic to
V(\lambda)
. Set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}_{M}[\lambda]:=\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M[\lambda]=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{k}=\lambda\end{subarray}}\mathcal{L}_{k},\\
&\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}[\lambda]:=\mathcal{L}_{M}[\lambda]/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M}[\lambda]=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{k}=\lambda\end{subarray}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k},\\
&\mathcal{B}_{M}[\lambda]:=\mathcal{B}_{M}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}[\lambda]=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{k}=\lambda\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}_{k}.\end{split}

Also, set

M[\geq\lambda]:=\bigoplus_{\mu\geq\lambda}M[\mu],\quad M[>\lambda]:=\bigoplus_{\mu>\lambda}M[\mu],

and define
\mathcal{L}_{M}[\geq\lambda],\mathcal{L}_{M}[>\lambda],\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}[\geq\lambda],\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}[>\lambda],\mathcal{B}_{M}[\geq\lambda],\mathcal{B}_{M}[>\lambda]
in the obvious way. Then,
M[\geq\lambda]
,
M[>\lambda]
, and
M[\geq\lambda]/M[>\lambda]
are
\mathbf{U}
-modules possessing crystal bases
\mathcal{B}_{M}[\geq\lambda]
,
\mathcal{B}_{M}[>\lambda]
, and
\{[b]\mid b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}[\lambda]\}
, respectively, where
[b]
denotes the image of
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}[\geq\lambda]
in
\overline{\mathcal{L}}[\geq\lambda]/\overline{\mathcal{L}}[>\lambda]
.

2.6.
Based
\mathbf{U}
-modules

Let
\dot{\mathbf{U}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\mu\in X}{}_{\lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mu}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}\dot{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
denote the modified quantum group, where the
\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
are the associated orthogonal idempotents (see
[
23
, Chapter 23]
for a precise definition).

Definition 2.6.1

(
[
23
, 23.1.4]
)
.

A unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
-module is a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
-module
M
such that for each
m\in M
, we have the following:

(1)

\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}m=0
for all but finitely many
\lambda\in X
.

(2)

\sum_{\lambda\in X}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}m=m
.

As explained in
[
23
, 23.1.4]
, a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module has a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
-module structure, and vice versa.

Set

\mathbf{A}:=\mathbb{C}[q,q^{-1}]\subseteq\mathbf{K}.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}
denote the
\mathbf{A}
-form of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
. It is an
\mathbf{A}
-subalgebra of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
generated by the divided powers
E_{i}^{(n)}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
,
F_{i}^{(n)}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda}
,
i\in I
,
\lambda\in X
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition 2.6.2
.

Let
M
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module. An
\mathbf{A}
-form of
M
is an
\mathbf{A}
-lattice
M_{\mathbf{A}}
of
M
which is simultaneously a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}
-submodule.

Definition 2.6.3
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module. A bar-involution
\psi_{M}
on
M
is an involutive
\mathbb{C}
-linear automorphism such that

\psi_{M}(xm)=\psi(x)\psi_{M}(m)\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U},\ m\in M.

Definition 2.6.4
.

Let
M
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
, an
\mathbf{A}
-form
M_{\mathbf{A}}
, a bar-involution
\psi_{M}
, and a crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
. We say that
(M,(\cdot,\cdot)_{M},M_{\mathbf{A}},\psi_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
, or simply
M
, is a based
\mathbf{U}
-module if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

The quotient map
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}

of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces; let
G:\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})
denote its inverse.

(2)

For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, we have
\psi_{M}(G(b))=G(b)
.

Remark 2.6.5
.

This definition is equivalent to
[
23
, 27.1.2]
.
We choose this definition so that we can straightforwardly generalize it to the
\imath
quantum groups setting.

Example 2.6.6
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
. Then, the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
possesses a unique bar-involution
\psi_{\lambda}
fixing the highest weight vector
v_{\lambda}
. Set
V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}:=\dot{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{A}}v_{\lambda}
. Then,
(V(\lambda),(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda},V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}},\psi_{\lambda},\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
is a based
\mathbf{U}
-module. The set
G(\lambda):=G(\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
is called the canonical basis of
V(\lambda)
.

A finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}
-module is cellular in the following sense.

Proposition 2.6.7

(
[
23
, Propositions 27.1.7 and 27.1.8]
)
.

Let
(M,(\cdot,\cdot)_{M},M_{\mathbf{A}},\psi_{M})
be a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}
-module, and
\mathcal{B}_{M}
a crystal basis. Then, for each
\lambda\in X^{+}
, the
\mathbf{U}
-modules
M[\geq\lambda]
,
M[>\lambda]
, and
M[\geq\lambda]/M[>\lambda]
are based
\mathbf{U}
-modules with the same (or induced) contragredient Hermitian inner products and bar-involutions as
M
, and the
\mathbf{A}
-forms spanned by
G(\mathcal{B}_{M}[\geq\lambda])
,
G(\mathcal{B}_{M}[>\lambda])
,
[G(\mathcal{B}_{M}[\lambda])]:=\{G(b)+M[>\lambda]\mid b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}[\lambda]\}
, respectively. Furthermore, there exists an isomorphism

M[\geq\lambda]/M[>\lambda]\rightarrow V(\lambda)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}

of
\mathbf{U}
-modules which restricts to a bijection

[G(\mathcal{B}_{M}[\lambda])]\rightarrow G(\lambda)^{m_{\lambda}}

between the canonical bases, where
m_{\lambda}:=\dim_{\mathbf{K}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{U}}(V(\lambda),M)
denotes the multiplicity of
V(\lambda)
in
M
.

3.
Quantum symmetric pairs

In this section, we formulate the
\imath
quantum groups following Kolb
[
19
]
. Then, we introduce the notion of based modules over the
\imath
quantum groups generalizing that over the quantum groups.

3.1.
\imath
Quantum groups

Let
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
be a Satake diagram, and
\mathfrak{k}
the associated symmetric pair subalgebra (see e.g.,
[
5
, Table 4]
for the list of Satake diagrams). Namely,
I_{\bullet}
is a subdiagram of
I
, and
\tau
is a diagram involution on
I
satisfying certain conditions (see
[
19
, Definition 2.3]
), and
\mathfrak{k}
is the Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by the following elements:

•

e_{i}
,
i\in I_{\bullet}
.

•

h^{\tau}_{i}:=\begin{cases}h_{i}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\bullet},\\
h_{i}-h_{\tau(i)}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\circ}.\end{cases}

•

b_{i}:=\begin{cases}f_{i}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\bullet},\\
f_{i}+\overline{\varsigma}_{i}\overline{T}_{w_{\bullet}}(e_{\tau(i)})\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\circ}.\end{cases}

Here,
I_{\circ}:=I\setminus I_{\bullet}
,
w_{\bullet}
is the longest element of the Weyl group of
I_{\bullet}
,
\overline{T}_{w_{\bullet}}
is the braid group action on
\mathfrak{g}
in terms of triple exponentials, and
\overline{\varsigma}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
are certain scalars. There exists a Lie algebra involution
\theta
on
\mathfrak{g}
such that

\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}^{\theta}:=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\theta(x)=x\}.

It is known that
\mathfrak{k}
is a reductive Lie algebra (
[
9
, Proposition 1.13.3]
).

Let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\boldsymbol{\varsigma},\boldsymbol{\kappa}}
denote the
\imath
quantum group associated with the Satake diagram
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
and parameters
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}=(\varsigma_{i})_{i\in I_{\circ}}\in(\mathbf{K}_{1}^{\times})^{I_{\circ}}
,
\boldsymbol{\kappa}=(\kappa_{i})_{i\in I_{\circ}}\in\mathbf{K}_{1}^{I_{\circ}}
obeying constraints
[
5
, Equations (3.4)–(3.8) and the assumption in Proposition 4.6]
. Namely,
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
E_{i}
,
i\in I_{\bullet}
,
B_{i},k_{i}
,
i\in I
where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&k_{i}^{\pm 1}:=\begin{cases}K_{i}^{\pm 1}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\bullet},\\
(K_{i}K_{\tau(i)}^{-1})^{\pm 1}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\circ},\end{cases}\\
&B_{i}:=\begin{cases}F_{i}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\bullet},\\
F_{i}+\varsigma_{i}T_{w_{\bullet}}(E_{\tau(i)})K_{i}^{-1}+\kappa_{i}K_{i}^{-1}\quad&\text{ if }i\in I_{\circ}.\end{cases}\end{split}

One of the most distinguished properties of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is that it is a right coideal of
\mathbf{U}
, i.e.,
\Delta(\mathbf{U}^{\imath})\subseteq\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\otimes\mathbf{U}
. Therefore, the tensor product of a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module and a
\mathbf{U}
-module becomes a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module via
\Delta
.

Proposition 3.1.1

(
[
5
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

For each
i\in I_{\circ}
, we have
\wp(B_{i})\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
, and

\wp(B_{i})=q_{i}^{-1}\varsigma_{\tau(i)}^{-1}T_{w_{\bullet}}^{-1}(B_{\tau(i)})K_{w_{\bullet}(h_{\tau(i)})}K_{i}^{-1}.

Consequently, the involution
\wp^{*}
on
\mathbf{U}
preserves
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
.

Proposition 3.1.2

(
[
2
, Theorem 3.11]
)
.

There exists a unique
\mathbb{C}
-algebra involution
\psi^{\imath}
, called the
\imath
bar-involution on
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
such that

\psi^{\imath}(E_{i})=E_{i},\quad\psi^{\imath}(B_{i})=B_{i},\quad\psi^{\imath}(k_{i})=k_{i}^{-1},\quad\psi^{\imath}(q)=q^{-1}.

3.2.
\imath
Canonical bases of based
\mathbf{U}
-modules

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module. An
\imath
bar-involution on
M
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear involution
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
on
M
such that

\psi^{\imath}_{M}(xm)=\psi^{\imath}(x)\psi^{\imath}_{M}(m)\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\and m\in M.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module equipped with a bar-involution
\psi_{M}
. Let
\Upsilon
denote the quasi-
K
-matrix (see
[
5
]
for a precise definition). Then,
\psi^{\imath}_{M}:=\Upsilon\circ\psi_{M}
becomes an
\imath
bar-involution of the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
. We call it the
\imath
bar-involution associated with
\psi_{M}
.

Theorem 3.2.2

(
[
5
, Theorem 5.7]
)
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, there exists a unique
G^{\imath}(b)\in\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying the following:

(1)

\psi^{\imath}_{M}(G^{\imath}(b))=G^{\imath}(b)
.

(2)

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(G^{\imath}(b))=b
.

We call
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
the
\imath
canonical basis associated with the canonical basis
G(\mathcal{B}_{M})
. When
\mathcal{B}_{M}=\mathcal{B}(\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in X^{+}
, we denote by
G^{\imath}(\lambda)
the
\imath
canonical basis associated with
G(\lambda)
.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}
-module. Let
M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}M_{k}
be the irreducible decomposition such that
\mathcal{B}_{M}=\bigsqcup_{k=1}^{r}\mathcal{B}_{k}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\mathcal{B}_{M}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M_{k}}
. Let
\lambda_{k}\in X^{+}
be such that
M_{k}\simeq V(\lambda_{k})
. Then, for each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
, we have

G^{\imath}(b)\in M_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{l}>\lambda_{k}\end{subarray}}M_{l}.

Proof.

From Proposition
2.6.7
, we see that

G(b^{\prime})\in M_{l}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l^{\prime}\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{l^{\prime}}>\lambda_{l}\end{subarray}}M_{l^{\prime}}.

for all
l\in[1,r]
,
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{l}
. Then, by the construction of
G^{\imath}(b)
in
[
5
, Theorem 5.7]
, we have

G^{\imath}(b)\in M_{k}+\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in[1,r]\\
\lambda_{l}>\lambda_{k}\end{subarray}}\bigoplus_{b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{l}}\mathbf{K}G(b^{\prime}).

Combining these two facts, we obtain the assertion.
∎

3.3.
Modified
\imath
quantum groups

Following
[
5
, 3.1]
, we set

(4)

\displaystyle X^{\imath}:=X/\{\lambda+w_{\bullet}\circ\tau(\lambda)\mid\lambda\in X\},\quad Y^{\imath}:=\{h\in Y\mid h+w_{\bullet}\circ\tau(h)=0\}.

Then, the perfect pairing on
Y\times X
induces a bilinear map
Y^{\imath}\times X^{\imath}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
given by

\langle h,\overline{\lambda}\rangle:=\langle h,\lambda\rangle,\quad h\in Y^{\imath},\lambda\in X,

where
\overline{\lambda}
denotes the image of
\lambda
in
X^{\imath}
.

Recall that
Q=\sum_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
is the root lattice. From the definition of quantum groups, we see that
\mathbf{U}
is a
Q
-graded algebra such that
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}
are homogeneous of degree
\alpha_{i},-\alpha_{i},0
, respectively. Set

Q^{\imath}:=\{\overline{\lambda}\mid\lambda\in Q\}\subseteq X^{\imath}.

Then,
\mathbf{U}
is
Q^{\imath}
-graded, and
E_{i},B_{i},k_{i}^{\pm 1}
are homogeneous of degree
\overline{\alpha_{i}},-\overline{\alpha_{i}},\overline{0}
, respectively. Hence,
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
is a
Q^{\imath}
-graded algebra. Let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\overline{\lambda})
denote the homogeneous part of degree
\overline{\lambda}\in Q^{\imath}
. For
\zeta\in X^{\imath}\setminus Q^{\imath}
, set
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\zeta):=0
. Then, we have

\mathbf{U}^{\imath}=\bigoplus_{\overline{\lambda}\in Q^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\overline{\lambda})=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\zeta).

For each
\zeta,\eta\in X^{\imath}
, let
\pi_{\zeta,\eta}
denote the composition

\pi_{\zeta,\eta}:\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\zeta-\eta)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{U}^{\imath}/(\sum_{h\in Y^{\imath}}(K_{h}-q^{\langle h,\zeta\rangle})\mathbf{U}^{\imath}+\sum_{h\in Y^{\imath}}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(K_{h}-q^{\langle h,\eta\rangle})),

and set
{}_{\zeta}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\eta}
to be the image of
\pi_{\zeta,\eta}
. Clearly, we have
{}_{\zeta}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\eta}=0
if
\zeta-\eta\notin Q^{\imath}
.

The modified
\imath
quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
is defined by

\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta,\eta\in X^{\imath}\\
\zeta-\eta\in Q^{\imath}\end{subarray}}{}_{\zeta}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\eta}=\bigoplus_{\zeta,\eta\in X^{\imath}}{}_{\zeta}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\eta}.

Remark 3.3.1
.

This definition slightly differs from the one in
[
5
]
. However, it is what the authors of
[
5
]
actually considered. The author would like to thank Weiqiang Wang for a fruitful discussion on this matter.

The modified
\imath
quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
admits an associative algebra structure just like
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
does (see
[
23
, Chapter 23]
). For each
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in X^{\imath}
and
x_{i}\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\zeta_{i}-\eta_{i})
, set

\pi_{\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}}(x_{1})\pi_{\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}}(x_{2}):=\delta_{\eta_{1},\zeta_{2}}\pi_{\zeta_{1},\eta_{2}}(x_{1}x_{2}).

For each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, set

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}:=\pi_{\zeta,\zeta}(1).

These
\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
’s form a family of orthogonal idempotents:

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}\mathbf{1}_{\eta}=\delta_{\zeta,\eta}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}.

Also, we have

{}_{\zeta}\mathbf{U}^{\imath}_{\eta}=\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}\mathbf{1}_{\eta}.

The modified
\imath
quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
is equipped with a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-bimodule structure defined as follows: For each
x,z\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
and
y\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
, set

xyz:=\sum_{\zeta,\eta\in X^{\imath}}(x\mathbf{1}_{\zeta})y(\mathbf{1}_{\eta}z).

Especially, we have for each
i\in I
and
j\in I_{\bullet}
,

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&E_{j}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}=\mathbf{1}_{\zeta+\overline{\alpha_{j}}}E_{j},\\
&B_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}=\mathbf{1}_{\zeta-\overline{\alpha_{i}}}B_{i},\\
&k_{i}^{\pm 1}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}=\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}k_{i}^{\pm 1}=q^{\pm\langle h^{\tau}_{i},\zeta\rangle}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}.\end{split}

Definition 3.3.2
.

A
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
is said to be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module if it admits an
X^{\imath}
-gradation

M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}

satisfying the following:

(1)

E_{j}M_{\zeta}\subseteq M_{\zeta+\overline{\alpha_{j}}}
for all
j\in I_{\bullet}
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

(2)

B_{i}M_{\zeta}\subseteq M_{\zeta-\overline{\alpha_{i}}}
for all
i\in I
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

(3)

k_{i}^{\pm 1}m=q^{\pm\langle h^{\tau}_{i},\zeta\rangle}m
for all
i\in I
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
,
m\in M_{\zeta}
.

The subspace
M_{\zeta}
(resp., a vector in
M_{\zeta}
) is referred to as the
X^{\imath}
-weight space (resp., an
X^{\imath}
-weight vector) of weight
\zeta
.

Example 3.3.3
.

Let
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda}
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, it admits an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure such that

M_{\zeta}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in X\\
\overline{\lambda}=\zeta\end{subarray}}M_{\lambda}

for all
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
. We call it the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure of
M
.

Remark 3.3.4
.

Since the pairing
Y^{\imath}\times X^{\imath}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is not perfect, there may exist
\zeta,\eta\in X^{\imath}
such that
\langle h,\zeta\rangle=\langle h,\eta\rangle
for all
h\in Y^{\imath}
, but
\zeta\neq\eta
. Hence, the condition “
k_{i}^{\pm 1}m=q^{\pm\langle h^{\tau}_{i},\zeta\rangle}m
for all
i\in I
” cannot ensure that
m\in M_{\zeta}
.

Definition 3.3.5
.

A unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module is a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module
M
such that for each
m\in M
, we have

(1)

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}m=0
for all but finitely many
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

(2)

\sum_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}m=m
.

As in the quantum groups case, each unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module is equipped with an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure, and vice versa.

Let
M
be a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module. Then, it admits a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure. This action is compatible with the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-bimodule structure of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
.

Definition 3.3.6
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module. A
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-submodule
N\subseteq M
is said to be an
X^{\imath}
-weight submodule if we have
N=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}(N\cap M_{\zeta})
.

The following is immediate from the definition.

Proposition 3.3.7
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module, and
N\subseteq M
an
X^{\imath}
-weight submodule.

(1)

N
itself is an
X^{\imath}
-weight module such that
N_{\zeta}=N\cap M_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

(2)

The quotient
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M/N
admits an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure such that
(M/N)_{\zeta}=M_{\zeta}/N=M_{\zeta}/N_{\zeta}
for all
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
.

Proposition 3.3.8
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module, and
N\subseteq M
a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-submodule. Suppose that
N
is generated by
X^{\imath}
-weight vectors of
M
. Then,
N
is an
X^{\imath}
-weight submodule.

Proof.

As one can easily see, a sum of
X^{\imath}
-weight submodules of
M
is an
X^{\imath}
-weight submodule of
M
. Hence, it suffices to show the assertion for the case when
N
is generated by a single
X^{\imath}
-weight vector
m\in M_{\eta}
for some
\eta\in X^{\imath}
. Let
\zeta^{\prime}\in Q^{\imath}
and
x\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(\zeta^{\prime})
. Then, by equation (
5
), we have

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}x=x\mathbf{1}_{\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}}.

Hence, it follows that

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}(xm)=x\mathbf{1}_{\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}}m=\delta_{\zeta-\zeta^{\prime},\eta}xm\in N.

Therefore,
N
is a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-submodule of
M
. Hence, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}N=N\cap M_{\zeta}.

On the other hand, since
\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
’s are orthogonal idempotents and
M
is a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module, we obtain

N=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}N.

This proves the assertion.
∎

The modified quantum group
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
admits a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-bimodule structure given as follows: For each
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
,
\zeta_{i}\in X^{\imath}
, and
y\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}
, set

(x_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{1}})y(\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{2}}x_{2}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{i}\in X\\
\overline{\lambda_{i}}=\zeta_{i}\end{subarray}}(x_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\lambda_{1}})y(\mathbf{1}_{\lambda_{2}}x_{2}).

In the right-hand side, we regard
x_{i}
as an element of
\mathbf{U}
via the inclusion
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}
.

Let
M
be a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
-module. Then, it admits a weight module structure
M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in X}M_{\lambda}
, and hence, the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure
M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}
(see Example
3.3.3
). Therefore, it admits a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module structure. This structure is compatible with the
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-bimodule structure of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}
.

More generally, let us consider a Satake subdiagram.

Definition 3.3.9
.

A Satake subdiagram of a Satake diagram
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
is a triple
(J,J_{\bullet},\sigma)
satisfying the following:

•

(J,J_{\bullet},\sigma)
itself is a Satake diagram.

•

J\subseteq I
,
J_{\bullet}\subseteq I_{\bullet}
,
\sigma(j)\in\{j,\tau(j)\}
for all
j\in J
.

•

w_{\bullet}(\alpha_{\tau(j)})=w_{\bullet}(J)(\alpha_{\sigma(j)})
for all
j\in J\cap I_{\circ}
.

Here and after,
A(J)
denotes an object
A
constructed from
(J,J_{\bullet},\sigma)
instead of
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
.

Let
(J,J_{\bullet},\sigma)
be a Satake subdiagram of our Satake diagram
(I,I_{\bullet},\tau)
, and
\boldsymbol{\varsigma}(J),\boldsymbol{\kappa}(J)
parameters for
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(J)
such that
\varsigma_{j}=\varsigma(J)_{j}
,
\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{j}=\boldsymbol{\kappa}(J)_{j}
for all
j\in J\cap I_{\circ}
.
We claim that
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(J)\subseteq\mathbf{U}
.
In fact, for each
j\in J\cap I_{\circ}
, we have

T_{w_{\bullet}}(E_{\tau(j)})=T_{w_{\bullet}(J)}T_{w_{\bullet}(J)^{-1}w_{\bullet}}(E_{\tau(j)})=T_{w_{\bullet}(J)}(E_{\sigma(j)}).

The last equality follows from the identity
w_{\bullet}(J)^{-1}w_{\bullet}(\alpha_{\tau(j)})=\alpha_{\sigma(j)}
and
[
15
, Proposition 8.20]
.
Hence, we obtain

B_{j}(J)=F_{j}-\varsigma(J)_{j}T_{w_{\bullet}(J)}(E_{\sigma(j)})K_{j}^{-1}=F_{j}-\varsigma_{j}T_{w_{\bullet}}(E_{\tau(j)})K_{j}^{-1}=B_{j}\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}.

Similar statements for the other generators are verified more easily.

Example 3.3.10
.

Let
I
be a Dynkin diagram, and consider two Satake diagrams
(I,\emptyset,\operatorname{id})
and
(I,I,-w_{0})
, where
-w_{0}
denotes the diagram automorphism induced by the longest element of the Weyl group of
I
. Namely, the first diagram consists of only black nodes, while the second one only white nodes with the same underlying Dynkin diagram
I
. Then,
(I,\emptyset,\operatorname{id})
is a Satake subdiagram of
(I,I,-w_{0})
.

For each
\lambda\in X
, define
\lambda^{\prime}\in X(J)
by

\langle h_{j},\lambda^{\prime}\rangle=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{2}\langle w_{\bullet}(J)(h_{j})-h_{j},\lambda\rangle\quad&\text{ if }j\in J_{\circ}\cap I_{\bullet},\\
\langle h_{j},\lambda\rangle\quad&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

By
[
19
, (5.6)]
, for each
j\in J_{\circ}
, we have

w_{\bullet}(J)(h_{j})-h_{j}=w_{\bullet}(J)(h_{\sigma(j)})-h_{\sigma(j)}\in\sum_{k\in J_{\bullet}}\mathbb{Z}h_{k}.

Then, it follows that

\langle h_{j},\lambda+w_{\bullet}\circ\tau(\lambda)\rangle=\langle h_{j},\lambda^{\prime}+w_{\bullet}(J)\circ\sigma(\lambda^{\prime})\rangle\quad\text{ for all }j\in J.

This shows that there exists a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map
\cdot|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}:X^{\imath}\rightarrow X^{\imath}(J)
making the following diagram commute

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\ \cdot\ }}
\scriptstyle{\cdot|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}}
\textstyle{X^{\imath}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}}
\textstyle{X(J)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\ \cdot\ }}
\textstyle{X^{\imath}(J),}

where the left vertical arrow
\cdot|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}:X\rightarrow X(J)
denotes the ordinary restriction map from the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{g}
to the one
\mathfrak{h}(J)
of
\mathfrak{g}(J)
. Then, as before,
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
admits a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(J)
-bimodule structure: For each
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(J)
,
\eta_{i}\in X^{\imath}(J)
, and
y\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
, set

(x_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\eta_{1}})y(\mathbf{1}_{\eta_{2}}x_{2})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{i}\in X^{\imath}\\
\zeta_{i}|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}=\eta_{i}\end{subarray}}(x_{1}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{1}})y(\mathbf{1}_{\zeta_{2}}x_{2}).

Let
M
be a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module. Then, it admits an
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure
M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}
, where
M_{\zeta}=\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}M
. By restriction, we obtain a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}(J)
-module structure on
M
. Furthermore, it becomes an
X^{\imath}(J)
-weight module such that
M=\bigoplus_{\eta\in X^{\imath}(J)}M_{\eta}
, where

M_{\eta}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\in X^{\imath}\\
\zeta|_{\mathfrak{h}(J)}=\eta\end{subarray}}M_{\zeta}.

Therefore,
M
admits a unital
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(J)
-module structure. Again, this structure is compatible with the
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}(J)
-bimodule structure of
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
.

3.4.
Based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules

Recall that
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
acts on each weight
\mathbf{U}
-module via the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure. Set

\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}:=\{x\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}\mid xV(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}\subseteq V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}}\ \text{ for all }\lambda\in X^{+}\}.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module. An
\mathbf{A}
-form of
M
is an
\mathbf{A}
-lattice
M_{\mathbf{A}}
of
M
which is simultaneously a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
-submodule.

Definition 3.4.2
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module equipped with a Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
. We say that
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
is contragredient if it satisfies

(6)

\displaystyle(xu,v)_{M}=(u,\wp^{*}(x)v)_{M}\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath},\ u,v\in M.

Remark 3.4.3
.

Each contragredient Hermitian inner product on a
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is a contragredient Hermitian inner product of the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
M
.

Definition 3.4.4
.

Let
M
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
, an
\mathbf{A}
-form
M_{\mathbf{A}}
, an
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
, and a basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
. We say that
(M,(\cdot,\cdot)_{M},M_{\mathbf{A}},\psi^{\imath}_{M},\mathcal{B}_{M})
, or simply
M
, is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(1)

The quotient map
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}:\mathcal{L}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}

of
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces; let
G^{\imath}:\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})
denote its inverse.

(2)

For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, we have
\psi^{\imath}_{M}(G^{\imath}(b))=G^{\imath}(b)
.

Example 3.4.5
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
, and consider the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
. Then, by Theorem
3.2.2
, the quintuple
(V(\lambda),(\cdot,\cdot)_{\lambda},V(\lambda)_{\mathbf{A}},\psi^{\imath}_{\lambda},\mathcal{B}(\lambda))
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

Let
M
be a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module and
v\in\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}
. Suppose that
\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)=v
. Then, we have

v=G^{\imath}(\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v)).

In particular, if
v\in q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M}
, then we have
v=0
.

Proof.

By our assumption that
v=\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)\in\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M})
, we have

v\in\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}\cap\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\mathcal{L}_{M}).

Since
G^{\imath}
is the inverse of
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}
, the assertion follows.
∎

Proposition 3.4.7
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module with
\mathcal{B}_{M}
being an orthonormal basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
. Then,
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
forms an almost orthonormal
\mathbf{K}
-basis of
M
, a free
\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
-basis of
\mathcal{L}_{M}
, and a free
\mathbf{A}
-basis of
M_{\mathbf{A}}
.

Proof.

Since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}=\dim_{\mathbf{K}}M
,
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
forms a
\mathbf{K}
-basis of
M
. For each
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, we have

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((G^{\imath}(b),G^{\imath}(b^{\prime}))_{M})=(\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(G^{\imath}(b)),\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(G^{\imath}(b^{\prime})))_{M}=(b,b^{\prime})_{M}=\delta_{b,b^{\prime}}.

This implies that
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
is almost orthonormal.
∎

Definition 3.4.8
.

Let
M,N
be based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules and
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}}(M,N)
.

(1)

We say that
f
is a homomorphism of based (
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-)modules if

f(G^{\imath}(b))\in G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{N})\sqcup\{0\}

for all
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
.

(2)

We say that
M
is a based submodule of
N
if
f
is the inclusion map and a homomorphism of based modules.

(3)

We say that
N
is a based quotient module of
M
if
f
is the quotient map and a homomorphism of based modules.

Proposition 3.4.9
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module with
\mathcal{B}_{M}
being an orthonormal basis, and
N\subseteq M
a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-submodule. Suppose that
N=\mathbf{K}G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{N})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{N}:=\mathcal{B}_{M}\cap\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N}
.

(1)

Set
N_{\mathbf{A}}:=N\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}
, and
\psi^{\imath}_{N}:=\psi^{\imath}_{M}|_{N}
. Then,
(N,(\cdot,\cdot)_{N},N_{\mathbf{A}},\psi^{\imath}_{N},\mathcal{B}_{N})
is a based submodule of
M
.

(2)

Let
\psi^{\imath}_{M/N}
denote the
\imath
bar-involution on
M/N
induced from
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{M/N}:=\{b+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N}\mid b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}{\setminus}\mathcal{B}_{N}\}
. Then,
(M/N,(\cdot,\cdot)_{M/N},M_{\mathbf{A}}/N_{\mathbf{A}},\psi^{\imath}_{M/N},\mathcal{B}_{M/N})
is a based quotient module of
M
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4.7
,
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{N})
and
G^{\imath}(\mathcal{B}_{M})
form
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
-invariant almost orthonormal free
\mathbf{A}
-bases of
N_{\mathbf{A}}
and
M_{\mathbf{A}}
, respectively. Furthermore,
\{[G^{\imath}(b)]\mid b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}\setminus\mathcal{B}_{N}\}
forms a
\psi^{\imath}_{M/N}
-invariant almost orthonormal free
\mathbf{A}
-basis of
M_{\mathbf{A}}/N_{\mathbf{A}}
. Then, the assertion follows if we prove that
N_{\mathbf{A}}
is a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
-submodule.

Let
b\in\mathcal{B}_{N}
and
x\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
. Then, we have
xG^{\imath}(b)\in N\cap M_{\mathbf{A}}=N_{\mathbf{A}}
. Thus, the proof completes.
∎

4.
\imath
Quantum group of type AI

In the remainder, we restrict our attention to the
\imath
quantum group of type AI with parameters
\varsigma_{i}=q^{-1}
and
\kappa_{i}=0
for all
i\in I_{\circ}
. It is an
\imath
quantum group associated with Satake diagram
(I,\emptyset,\operatorname{id})
, where
I=[1,n-1]
is the Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n-1}
. We assume that
n\geq 3
unless otherwise specified. The case when
n=2
will be studied separately because
\mathfrak{k}
is not a simple Lie algebra in this case.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}(I)
denote the complex simple Lie algebra associated with
I
. Recall that
Y=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z}h_{i}
and
X=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{i}
are the coroot lattice and the weight lattice for
\mathfrak{g}
. We often identify
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{i}\varpi_{i}\in X
with
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
. For each
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1})\in X
, we have
\lambda\in X^{+}
if and only if
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in I
.

4.1.
Symmetric pair of type AI

Let
\mathfrak{k}
be the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
generated by
b_{i}:=f_{i}+e_{i}
,
i\in I
. The defining relations of
\mathfrak{k}
for the generators
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-1}
are as follows (see
[
28
, Definition 2.5 and Theorem 2.7]
):

(7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[b_{i},b_{j}]=0\quad\text{ if }|i-j|>1,\\
&[b_{i},[b_{i},b_{j}]]=b_{j}\quad\text{ if }|i-j|=1.\end{split}

Let us identify
\mathfrak{g}
with the special linear Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}:=\{X\in\operatorname{Mat}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid\operatorname{tr}X=0\}
in the usual way. Consider the special orthogonal Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}_{n}:=\{X\in\mathfrak{g}\mid X+{}^{t}X=0\}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
. It is generated by
b^{\prime}_{i}:=f_{i}-e_{i}
,
i\in I
. Let
m
denote the rank of
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
:

m=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}\quad&\text{ if }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\frac{n-1}{2}\quad&\text{ if }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

Then,
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{C}b^{\prime}_{2i-1}
forms a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{so}_{n}
since it forms an abelian subalgebra of dimension
m
.

Set
I_{\mathfrak{k}}:=[1,m]
, and identify it with the Dynkin diagram of type
D_{m}
if
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}
or
B_{m}
if
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
whose vertices are labeled by
I_{\mathfrak{k}}
in the same manner as
[
13
, Section 11.4]
. Let
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i},h^{\prime}_{i}
,
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
be the Chevalley generators of
\mathfrak{g}(I_{\mathfrak{k}})
.

Let
I_{\otimes}:=I\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\{2i-1\mid i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}\}
, and set
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
to be the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
generated by
b_{i}
,
i\in I_{\otimes}
. As before, we see that
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}
is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{k}
. Let
\{b^{i}\mid i\in I_{\otimes}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}
denote the dual basis of
\{b_{i}\mid i\in I_{\otimes}\}
. For each
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
, set

\gamma_{i}:=\begin{cases}b^{2i-1}-b^{2i+1}\quad&\text{ if }i\neq m,\\
b^{2m-3}+b^{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
b^{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

For each
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
2i<n
, set

b_{2i,\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(b_{2i}\pm[b_{2i-1},b_{2i}]).

Also, for each
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
2i<n-1
and for each
e\in\{+,-\}
, set

b_{2i,e\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(b_{2i,e}\pm[b_{2i+1},b_{2i,e}]).

Now, for each
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
, set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&x_{i}:=\begin{cases}2b_{2i,+-}\quad&\text{ if }i\neq m,\\
2b_{2m-2,++}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
2b_{2m,+}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}},\end{cases}\\
&y_{i}:=\begin{cases}2b_{2i,-+}\quad&\text{ if }i\neq m,\\
2b_{2m-2,--}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
2b_{2m,-}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}},\end{cases}\\
&w_{i}:=\begin{cases}b_{2i-1}-b_{2i+1}\quad&\text{ if }i\neq m,\\
b_{2m-3}+b_{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
2b_{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Using relations (
7
), one can verify that there exists an isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}(I_{\mathfrak{k}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{k}
of Lie algebras sending
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i},h^{\prime}_{i}
to
x_{i},y_{i},w_{i}
, respectively (see also
[
31
, Sections 4.1–4.2]
).

Define
x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{so}_{n}
,
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
in the same way as
x_{i},y_{i},w_{i}
by replacing
b_{i}
with
\sqrt{-1}b^{\prime}_{i}
. Then, there exists an isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}(I_{\mathfrak{k}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{so}_{n}
of Lie algebras sending
e^{\prime}_{i},f^{\prime}_{i},h^{\prime}_{i}
to
x^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i}
, respectively. However, one should note that
\mathfrak{so}_{n}\neq\mathfrak{k}
set-theoretically.

Set
Y_{\mathfrak{k}}:=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}}\mathbb{Z}w_{i}
,
X_{\mathfrak{k}}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Y_{\mathfrak{k}},\mathbb{Z})
,
X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}:=\{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}\mid\langle w_{i},\nu\rangle\geq 0\text{ for all }i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}\}
. Then, the isoclasses of finite-dimensional irreducible
\mathfrak{k}
-modules are parametrized by
X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
. Let
V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu)
denote the irreducible
\mathfrak{k}
-module of highest weight
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
.

We often identify
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
with
(\nu_{1},\nu_{3},\ldots,\nu_{2m-1})\in(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z})^{I_{\otimes}}
, where

\nu_{i}:=\langle b_{i},\nu\rangle.

Here, we extend
\nu
to a linear form on
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}=Y_{\mathfrak{k}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
. With this notation, we have

\langle w_{i},\nu\rangle=\begin{cases}\nu_{2i-1}-\nu_{2i+1}\quad&\text{ if }i\neq m,\\
\nu_{2m-3}+\nu_{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
2\nu_{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }i=m\and n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

Note that we have either
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{I_{\otimes}}
or
\nu\in(\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z})^{I_{\otimes}}
. We call
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{I_{\otimes}}
an integer weight. Set
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
to be the set of integer weights. Let
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{3},\ldots,\nu_{2m-1})\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
. When
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}
, we have
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
if and only if
\nu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{2m-3}\geq|\nu_{2m-1}|
, while when
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, we have
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
if and only if
\nu_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\nu_{2m-1}\geq 0
.

4.2.
\imath
Quantum group of type AI

Let
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
denote the
\imath
quantum group of type AI with parameters
\varsigma_{i}=q^{-1}
,
\kappa_{i}=0
for all
i\in I
. It is a subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
B_{i}=F_{i}+q^{-1}E_{i}K_{i}^{-1}
,
i\in I
. The defining relations of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
for
B_{1},\ldots,B_{n-1}
are as follows (see e.g.
[
19
, Section 7]
):

(8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[B_{i},B_{j}]=0\quad\text{ if }|i-j|>1,\\
&[B_{i},[B_{i},B_{j}]_{q}]_{q^{-1}}=B_{j}\quad\text{ if }|i-j|=1.\end{split}

Let us recall the notion of classical weight
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules, which was introduced in
[
31
]
.

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module.
M
is said to be a classical weight module if for each
i\in I_{\otimes}
,
B_{i}
acts on
M
diagonally with eigenvalues belonging to
\mathbf{K}_{1}
.

Let
M
be a classical weight module. For each
\nu\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}
, set
M_{\nu}
to be the subspace of
M
consisting of
v\in M
satisfying the following: For each
i\in I_{\otimes}
,
B_{i}v=av
for some
a\in\mathbf{K}_{1}
such that
\mathrm{ev}_{1}(a)=\langle b_{i},\nu\rangle
. Then, we have by definition

M=\bigoplus_{\nu\in\mathfrak{h}_{\mathfrak{k}}^{*}}M_{\nu}.

The subspace
M_{\nu}
(resp., an element of
M_{\nu}
) is referred to as the
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight space of
M
(resp., an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector) of weight
\nu
.

Proposition 4.2.2

(
[
31
, Proposition 3.1.4 and Remark 3.1.5]
)
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}
-module of finite dimension. Then, as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module, it is a classical weight module. Moreover, for each
i\in I_{\otimes}
, the eigenvalues of
B_{i}
are of the form
[a]
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proposition 4.2.3

(
[
31
, Proposition 3.1.6]
)
.

Let
M
be a classical weight module, and
N
a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then,
M\otimes N
is a classical weight module.

Remark 4.2.4
.

A weight vector of a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module
M
is not necessarily an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector of
M
. Similarly, the tensor product of an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}-
weight vector of a classical weight module
M
and a weight vector of a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module
N
is not necessarily an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector of
M\otimes N
.

In
[
31
]
, we defined linear operators
l_{j}^{\pm 1}
for
j\in I_{\otimes}
, and
B_{i,e}
,
B_{i,e_{1}e_{2}}
for
i\in I\setminus I_{\otimes}
,
e,e_{1},e_{2}\in\{+,-\}
acting on each classical weight module
M
. By definition, for each
v\in M
and
e\in\{+,-\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&l_{j}^{\pm 1}v=q^{\pm a}v\quad\text{ if }B_{j}v=[a]v,\\
&B_{2i,\pm}=(B_{2i}l_{2i-1}^{\pm 1}\pm[B_{2i-1},B_{2i}]_{q})\frac{1}{\{l_{2i-1};0\}},\\
&B_{2i,e\pm}=(B_{2i,e}l_{2i+1}^{\pm 1}\pm[B_{2i+1},B_{2i,e}]_{q})\frac{1}{\{l_{2i+1};0\}}.\end{split}

Set

\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}:=\begin{cases}\{(2i,\pm)\mid i\in[1,m-1]\}\quad&\text{ if }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\{(2i,\pm)\mid i\in[1,m-1]\}\sqcup\{2m\}\quad&\text{ if }n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

For each
j\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&X_{j}:=\begin{cases}B_{2i,+\pm}\{l_{2i-1};0\}\quad&\text{ if }j=(2i,\pm),\\
B_{2m,+}\{l_{2m-1}\}\quad&\text{ if }j=2m,\end{cases}\\
&Y_{j}:=\begin{cases}B_{2i,-\mp}\{l_{2i+1};0\}\quad&\text{ if }j=(2i,\pm),\\
B_{2m,-}\{l_{2m-1}\}\quad&\text{ if }j=2m,\end{cases}\\
&\gamma_{j}:=\begin{cases}b^{2i-1}\pm b^{2i+1}\quad&\text{ if }j=(2i,\pm),\\
b^{2m-1}\quad&\text{ if }j=2m.\end{cases}\end{split}

For each
\nu,\xi\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we write
\xi\leq\nu
to indicate that
\nu-\xi\in\sum_{i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\gamma_{i}=\sum_{j\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\gamma_{j}
. This defines a partial order on
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
, called the dominance order.

Theorem 4.2.5

(
[
31
, Corollaries 4.1.4, 4.1.7, 4.2.2, and 4.2.4]
)
.

(1)

For each
i\in I_{\otimes}
,
l_{i}
(resp.,
B_{i}
) acts on each finite-dimensional classical weight module diagonally with eigenvalues of the form
q^{a}
(resp.,
[a]
),
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

For each classical weight module
M
,
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
, and
j\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we have

X_{j}M_{\nu}\subseteq M_{\nu+\gamma_{j}},\quad Y_{j}M_{\nu}\subseteq M_{\nu-\gamma_{j}}.

(3)

Each finite-dimensional classical weight module is completely reducible.

(4)

The isoclasses of finite-dimensional irreducible classical weight modules are parametrized by the set
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
: The finite-dimensional irreducible classical weight module
V(\nu)
corresponding to
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
is characterized by the condition that there exists a unique (up to nonzero scalar multiple)
v_{\nu}\in V(\nu)_{\nu}\setminus\{0\}
such that
X_{j}v_{\nu}=0
for all
j\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}
; the vector
v_{\nu}
is referred to as a highest weight vector.

(5)

For each
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
, we have

V(\nu)=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbf{K}}\{Y_{j_{1}}\cdots Y_{j_{r}}v_{\nu}\mid r\geq 0,\ j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}\}.

(6)

For each
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
and
\xi\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we have
\dim V(\nu)_{\nu}=1
, and
\dim V(\nu)_{\xi}=0
unless
\xi\leq\nu
.

(7)

For each
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
and
\xi\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
\xi<\nu
, we have

V(\nu)_{\xi}=\sum_{j\in\widetilde{I}_{\mathfrak{k}}}Y_{j}V(\nu)_{\xi+\gamma_{j}}.

Given a finite-dimensional classical weight module
M
and
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{3},\ldots,\nu_{2m-1})\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
, the weight space
M_{\nu}
is described as

M_{\nu}=\{v\in M\mid l_{i}v=q^{\nu_{i}}v\text{ for all }i\in I_{\otimes}\}=\{v\in M\mid B_{i}v=[\nu_{i}]v\text{ for all }i\in I_{\otimes}\}.

Define the character
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}M\in\mathbb{Z}[X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}]
of
M
by

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}M:=\sum_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}(\dim_{\mathbf{K}}M_{\nu})e^{\nu}.

Proposition 4.2.6
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. Then, we have

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}V(\nu)=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu),

where the right-hand side denotes the character of the irreducible
\mathfrak{k}
-module
V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu)
of highest weight
\nu
.

Proof.

The assertion essentially follows from
[
31
, arguments before Proposition 3.3.9]
.
∎

Let us recall from
[
31
]
some formulas which will be frequently used below.

(9)

\displaystyle B_{2i-1}=[l_{2i-1};0],

(10)

\displaystyle B_{2i,\pm}=(B_{2i}l_{2i-1}^{\pm 1}\pm[B_{2i-1},B_{2i}]_{q})\frac{1}{\{l_{2i-1};0\}},

(11)

\displaystyle B_{2i}=B_{2i,+}+B_{2i,-},

(12)

\displaystyle l_{2j-1}B_{2i,\pm}=q^{\pm\delta_{i,j}}B_{2i,\pm}l_{2j-1}\quad\text{ if }j\neq i+1,

(13)

\displaystyle[B_{2i,+}\{l_{2i-1};0\},B_{2i,-}\{l_{2i-1};0\}]=[l_{i}^{2};0],

(14)

\displaystyle B_{2i,e\pm}=(B_{2i,e}l_{2i+1}^{\pm 1}\pm[B_{2i+1},B_{2i,e}]_{q})\frac{1}{\{l_{2i+1};0\}},

(15)

\displaystyle B_{2i,\pm}=B_{2i,\pm+}+B_{2i,\pm-},

(16)
