\displaystyle l_{2j-1}B_{2i,e_{1}e_{2}}=q^{e_{1}\delta_{i,j}+e_{2}\delta_{i,j-1}}B_{2i,e_{1}e_{2}}l_{2j-1},

(17)

\displaystyle[B_{2i,+\pm}\{l_{2i-1};0\},B_{2i,-\mp}\{l_{2i+1};0\}]=(1+(q-q^{-1})^{2}B_{2i,-\pm}B_{2i,+\mp})[l_{2i-1}l_{2i+1}^{\pm 1};0],

(18)

\displaystyle[B_{2i,+\pm}\{l_{2i-1};0\},B_{2i,-\pm}\{l_{2i-1};0\}]=0,

(19)

\displaystyle[B_{2i,\pm+}\{l_{2i+1};0\},B_{2i,\pm-}\{l_{2i+1};0\}]=0.

Lemma 4.2.7
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Then, for each
u,v\in M
,
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we have (if
B_{2i,\pm}
, etc. is defined)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(l_{2i-1}u,v)=(u,l_{2i-1}v),\\
&(B_{2i,\pm}u,v)=(u,B_{2i,\mp}v),\\
&(B_{2i,+\pm}u,v)=(u,B_{2i,-\mp}v),\\
&(B_{2i,-\pm}u,v)=(u,B_{2i,+\mp}v).\end{split}

Proof.

The first assertion is reduced to the case when both
u
and
v
are
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vectors. The latter is clear from the definitions.

Applying Proposition
3.1.1
to our case, we have

\wp^{*}(B_{i})=B_{i}

for all
i\in I
. Then, the remaining assertions are directly verified by using the first assertion and equations (
9
)–(
16
).
∎

Lemma 4.2.8
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with an
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
. Then, for each
v\in M
,
i\in I_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we have (if
B_{2i,\pm}
, etc. is defined)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi^{\imath}_{M}(l_{2i-1}v)=l_{2i-1}^{-1}\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v),\\
&\psi^{\imath}_{M}(B_{2i,\pm}v)=B_{2i,\pm}\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v),\\
&\psi^{\imath}_{M}(B_{2i,+\pm}v)=B_{2i,+\pm}\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v),\\
&\psi^{\imath}_{M}(B_{2i,-\pm}v)=B_{2i,-\pm}\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v).\end{split}

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
4.2.7
.
∎

4.3.
Contragredient Hermitian inner products and
\imath
bar-involutions on irreducible modules

In this subsection, we construct a contragredient Hermitian inner product and an
\imath
bar-involution on
V(\nu)
,
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
.

Let
V_{\natural}
denote the natural representation of
\mathbf{U}
. Namely, it possesses a basis
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{n}\}
such that

E_{i}u_{j}=\delta_{i,j-1}u_{i},\quad F_{i}u_{j}:=\delta_{i,j}u_{i+1},\quad K_{i}u_{j}:=q^{\delta_{i,j}-\delta_{i,j-1}}u_{j}.

The natural representation
V_{\natural}
is equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\natural}
given by

(u_{i},u_{j})_{\natural}=\delta_{i,j}.

Set

\mathcal{L}_{\natural}:=\mathcal{L}_{V_{\natural}},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\natural}:=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V_{\natural}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{\natural}:=\{\overline{u}_{1},\ldots,\overline{u}_{n}\},

where
\overline{u}_{i}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(u_{i})
. Note that
\mathcal{B}_{\natural}
forms a crystal basis of
V_{\natural}
on which the Kashiwara operators act as

\widetilde{E}_{i}\overline{u}_{j}=\delta_{i,j-1}\overline{u}_{i},\quad\widetilde{F}_{i}\overline{u}_{j}=\delta_{i,j}\overline{u}_{i+1}.

The following follows from a direct calculation.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module,
v\in M
. Suppose that
B_{i}v=[a_{i}]v
for some
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
i\in I_{\otimes}
. Let
i\in I_{\otimes}
, and set
v^{\prime}_{i,\pm}:=v\otimes(u_{i}\pm q^{\pm a_{i}}u_{i+1})
. Also, set
v_{0}:=v\otimes u_{n}
if
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
. Then, for each
j\in I_{\otimes}
, we have

B_{j}v^{\prime}_{i,\pm}=[a_{j}\pm\delta_{i,j}]v^{\prime}_{i,\pm},\quad B_{j}v_{0}=[a_{j}]v_{0}.

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Let
d\geq 0
and
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
. Set
M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
.

(1)

If
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}
, then,
\dim M_{\nu}
equals the number of sequences

(e_{1},\ldots,e_{d})\in\{(i,+),(i,-)\mid i\in I_{\otimes}\}^{d}

such that
\sharp\{k\mid e_{k}=(i,+)\}-\sharp\{k\mid e_{k}=(i,-)\}=\nu_{i}
for all
i\in I_{\otimes}
.

(2)

If
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}
, then,
\dim M_{\nu}
equals the number of sequences

(e_{1},\ldots,e_{d})\in\{(i,+),(i,-),0\mid i\in I_{\otimes}\}^{d}

such that
\sharp\{k\mid e_{k}=(i,+)\}-\sharp\{k\mid e_{k}=(i,-)\}=\nu_{i}
for all
i\in I_{\otimes}
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
4.3.1
, the assertions follow by induction on
d
.
∎

Let
d\geq 0
and set
M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
. Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{M}
denote the contragredient Hermitian inner product on
M
obtained from
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\natural}
by means of Lemma
2.4.3
. Set

M_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}\{u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{d}}\mid i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\in[1,n]\}.

Then,
M_{1}:=M_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}/(q-1)M_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}
is a
\mathfrak{g}(=\mathfrak{sl}_{n})
-module isomorphic to
(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes d}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is equipped with the natural
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
-module structure. As a
\mathfrak{k}
-module, we have an irreducible decomposition

M_{1}\simeq\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}}V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu)^{\oplus m_{\nu}}

for some
m_{\nu}\geq 0
.

On the other hand, by induction on
d
and Lemma
4.3.1
, we see that each
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight space
M_{\nu}
,
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}
is spanned by vectors in
M_{\mathbf{A}_{1}}
. Therefore, we obtain

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}M=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}M_{1}=\sum_{\nu}m_{\nu}\mathrm{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu).

Furthermore, we have an irreducible decomposition

M\simeq\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}}V(\nu)^{\oplus m^{\prime}_{\nu}}

as a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module for some
m^{\prime}_{\nu}\geq 0
. Then, we have

\sum_{\nu}m_{\nu}\mathrm{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu)=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}M=\sum_{\nu}m^{\prime}_{\nu}\mathrm{ch}_{\mathbf{U}^{\imath}}V(\nu)=\sum_{\nu}m^{\prime}_{\nu}\mathrm{ch}_{\mathfrak{k}}V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu).

Since the irreducible characters of
\mathfrak{k}
are linearly independent, we conclude that
m_{\nu}=m^{\prime}_{\nu}
for all
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
.

Proposition 4.3.3
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. Then, there exists
d\geq 0
such that
V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
possesses a highest weight vector of weight
\nu
.

Proof.

It is a classical result that the irreducible
\mathfrak{k}
-module
V_{\mathfrak{k}}(\nu)
of highest weight
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
appears as a submodule of
(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{\otimes d}
for some
d\geq 0
. This implies, by the argument above, that the irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(\nu)
appears as a submodule of
V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
.

(1)

There exists a unique contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nu}
on
V(\nu)
such that
(v_{\nu},v_{\nu})_{\nu}=1
.

(2)

There exists a unique
\imath
bar-involution
\psi^{\imath}_{\nu}
on
V(\nu)
such that
\psi^{\imath}_{\nu}(v_{\nu})=v_{\nu}
.

Proof.

Let
d\geq 0
be such that
M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
possesses a highest weight vector
v
of weight
\nu
(see Proposition
4.3.3
). By Lemma
4.2.8
, we see that
\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)
is also a highest weight vector of weight
\nu
. Replacing
v
with
v+\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)
, we may assume that
\psi^{\imath}_{M}(v)=v
. Furthermore, replacing
v
with
\frac{1}{\operatorname{lt}(v)}v
, we may assume that
v
is almost normal. Let
\phi:V(\nu)\rightarrow M
be an
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-isomorphism such that
\phi(v_{\nu})=v
. Define maps
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nu}:V(\nu)\times V(\nu)\rightarrow\mathbf{K}
and
\psi^{\imath}_{\nu}:V(\nu)\rightarrow V(\nu)
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(u_{1},u_{2})_{\nu}:=(v,v)_{M}^{-1}\cdot(\phi(u_{1}),\phi(u_{2}))_{M},\\
&\psi^{\imath}_{\nu}(u):=\phi^{-1}(\psi^{\imath}_{M}(\phi(u))).\end{split}

Then, it is straightforwardly verified that these are a desired Hermitian inner product and an
\imath
bar-involution.

The uniqueness is easily verified. Hence, the proof completes.
∎

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. As in the quantum groups setting, with respect to the contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nu}
, we write

\mathcal{L}(\nu):=\mathcal{L}_{V(\nu)},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\nu):=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{V(\nu)},\quad b_{\nu}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v_{\nu}).

4.4.
\imath
Divided powers

Following
[
7
]
, for each
i\in I
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{i,\mathrm{ev}}^{(k)}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{[2a]!}B_{i}\prod_{b=-a+1}^{a-1}(B_{i}-[2b])\quad&\text{ if }k=2a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\frac{1}{[2a+1]!}\prod_{b=-a}^{a}(B_{i}-[2b])\quad&\text{ if }k=2a+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}},\\
\end{cases}\\
&B_{i,\mathrm{odd}}^{(k)}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{[2a]!}\prod_{b=-a+1}^{a}(B_{i}-[2b-1])\quad&\text{ if }k=2a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\frac{1}{[2a+1]!}B_{i}\prod_{b=-a+1}^{a}(B_{i}-[2b-1])\quad&\text{ if }k=2a+1\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proposition 4.4.1

(
[
7
, equations (2.5) and (3.2)]
)
.

For each
p\in\{\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{odd}\}
and
k\geq 0
, we have

B_{i}B_{i,p}^{(k)}=[k+1]B_{i,p}^{(k+1)}+\delta_{p,p(k)}[k]B_{i,p}^{(k-1)},

where we understand
B_{i,p}^{(-1)}=0
.

Recall
X^{\imath}
from equation (
4
) in page
4
. In our setting, we have

X^{\imath}=X/2X=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{n-1}.

For each
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-1})\in X^{\imath}
, set

B_{i,\zeta}^{(k)}:=B_{i,p(\zeta_{i})}^{(k)}\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}.

These elements are called
\imath
divided powers.

Theorem 4.4.2

(
[
6
, Corollary 7.5]
)
.

\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}
is generated by
B_{i,\zeta}^{(k)}
,
i\in I
,
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}
be an
X^{\imath}
-weight module. We say that
M
is standard if for each
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n-1})\in X^{\imath}
, we have

M_{\zeta}=\{v\in M\mid B_{i,p(\zeta_{i})}^{(k_{i})}v=0\text{ for some }k_{i}>0\text{ such that }\overline{k_{i}}\neq\zeta_{i},\text{ for all }i\in I\}.

The following two propositions are immediate from the definition.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
M
be a standard
X^{\imath}
-weight module, and
N\subseteq M
an
X^{\imath}
-weight submodule. Then,
N
is standard.

Proposition 4.4.5
.

Let
M
and
N
be standard
X^{\imath}
-weight modules. Then, each
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism
f:M\rightarrow N
preserves the
X^{\imath}
-weight spaces. In particular, it lifts to a
\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to proving that each finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module with the canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure is standard.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

Let
M
be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module. Let
v\in M
,
i\in I
, and
p\in\{\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{odd}\}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(1)

B_{i,p}^{(k)}v=0\text{ for some }k>0\text{ such that }p(k)\neq p
.

(2)

v
is a sum of
B_{i}
-eigenvectors of eigenvalues of the form
[a]
with
a\in[-k+1,k-1]_{p}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\imath
divided powers, if
p(k)\neq p
, then we have

B_{i,p}^{(k)}=\prod_{a\in[-k+1,k-1]_{p}}(B_{i}-[a]).

This implies the assertion.
∎

Proposition 4.4.7
.

Let
M
be a weight
\mathbf{U}
-module. Then, its canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure is standard.

Proof.

Let
i\in I
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and consider the
(l+1)
-dimensional irreducible
\mathbf{U}_{i}
-module
V(l)
, where
\mathbf{U}_{i}
denotes the subalgebra of
\mathbf{U}
generated by
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1}
. Let
v\in V(l)
be a highest weight vector. By
[
7
, Theorems 2.10 and 3.6]
,
V(l)
has a basis
\{B_{i,p(l)}^{(k)}v\mid k\in[0,l]\}
, and, we have
B_{i,p(l)}^{(l+1)}v=0
. Since
B_{i,p(l)}^{(k)}
’s commute with each other, we obtain

B_{i,p(l)}^{(l+1)}u=0\text{ for all }u\in V(l).

By Lemma
4.4.6
, we see that each vector in
V(l)
is a sum of
B_{i}
-eigenvectors of eigenvalues of the form
[a]
,
a\in[-l,l]_{p(l)}
.

Let
\lambda\in X
,
v\in M_{\lambda}
, and
i\in I
. We show that
B_{i,p(\lambda_{i})}^{(k_{i})}v=0
for some
k_{i}>0
such that
\overline{k_{i}}\neq\overline{\lambda_{i}}
. As a
\mathbf{U}_{i}
-module,
M
decomposes as

M=\bigoplus_{l\geq 0}M[l],

where
M[l]
denotes the isotypic component of
M
of type
V(l)
. By weight consideration, we have
v\in\bigoplus_{l\in\mathbb{Z}_{p(\lambda_{i})}}M[l]
. By argument above,
v
is a sum of
B_{i}
-eigenvectors of eigenvalues of the form
[a]
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p(\lambda_{i})}
. By Lemma
4.4.6
, we have
B_{i,p(\lambda_{i})}^{(k_{i})}v=0
for some
k_{i}>0
such that
\overline{k_{i}}\neq\overline{\lambda_{i}}
.

For each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, set

M^{\prime}_{\zeta}:=\{v\in M\mid B_{i,p(\zeta_{i})}^{(k_{i})}v=0\text{ for some }k_{i}>0\text{ such that }\overline{k_{i}}\neq\zeta_{i}\text{ for all }i\in I\}.

By above, we have

M_{\zeta}=\bigoplus_{\overline{\lambda}=\zeta}M_{\lambda}\subseteq M^{\prime}_{\zeta}\subseteq M.

Summing up through
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, we obtain

M=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M_{\zeta}=\sum_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}M^{\prime}_{\zeta}.

Since
M^{\prime}_{\zeta}
’s intersects trivially with each other, we conclude that

M_{\zeta}=M^{\prime}_{\zeta}.

This proves the assertion.
∎

4.5.
Classical weight modules with contragredient Hermitian inner products

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. The following are easy analogs of Lemma
2.4.4
and Proposition
2.4.5
.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

Let
\nu\neq\xi\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
. Then, we have
(M_{\nu},M_{\xi})=0
. Consequently, we have
\mathcal{L}_{M}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}\mathcal{L}_{M,\nu}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\nu}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{M,\nu}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\nu}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\nu}:=\mathcal{L}_{M,\nu}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M,\nu}
.

Proposition 4.5.2
.

There exists an orthogonal irreducible decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{r}M_{k}
of
M
.

Because of Proposition
4.5.2
, we can define
M[\nu],M[\geq\nu],M[>\nu]
, etc. in the same way as in the quantum groups setting.

Suppose further that
M
is a standard
X^{\imath}
-weight module. Then, its distinct
X^{\imath}
-weight spaces are orthogonal to each other:

Lemma 4.5.3
.

For each
\zeta\neq\eta\in X^{\imath}
, we have
(M_{\zeta},M_{\eta})=0
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\zeta\neq\eta
. Then, there exists
i\in I
such that
\zeta_{i}\neq\eta_{i}
. Let
u\in M_{\zeta}
and
v\in M_{\eta}
. By Lemma
4.4.6
, we can write
u=\sum_{k}u_{k}
(resp.,
v=\sum_{l}v_{l}
) with
B_{i}u_{k}=[a_{k}]u_{k}
,
\overline{a_{k}}=\zeta_{i}
(resp.,
B_{i}v_{l}=[b_{l}]v_{l}
,
\overline{b_{l}}=\eta_{i}
). Then, for each
k,l
, we have

[a_{k}](u_{k},v_{l})=(B_{i}u_{k},v_{l})=(u_{k},B_{i}v_{l})=[b_{l}](u_{k},v_{l}).

Since
a_{k}\neq b_{l}
, it follows that
(u_{k},v_{l})=0
. Hence, we obtain
(u,v)=\sum_{k,l}(u_{k},v_{l})=0
, as required.
∎

From this lemma together with Proposition
2.1.8
, we obtain orthogonal decompositions

\mathcal{L}_{M}=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta},\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}=\bigoplus_{\zeta\in X^{\imath}}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\zeta},

where
\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta}:=\mathcal{L}_{M}\cap M_{\zeta}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\zeta}:=\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta}/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{M,\zeta}
. Hence, for each
\zeta\in X^{\imath}
, the idempotent
\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}
defines a projection

\mathbf{1}_{\zeta}:\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\zeta}.

5.
n=2
case

Sections
5
–
7
are devoted to investigating low rank cases;
n=2,3,4
. The results obtained in these sections will be used to prove our main theorems.

In this section, we consider the
n=2
case. In this case, we can identify
X=\mathbb{Z}
,
X^{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
X^{\imath}=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Set
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}:=\mathbb{Z}
. For each
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
, let
\mathbf{K}_{\nu}=\mathbf{K}v_{\nu}
denote the
1
-dimensional
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module such that
B_{1}v_{\nu}=[\nu]v_{\nu}
. Clearly,
\mathbf{K}_{\nu}
possesses a unique contragredient Hermitian inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nu}
such that
(v_{\nu},v_{\nu})_{\nu}=1
. Set
\mathcal{L}(\nu):=\mathbf{K}_{\infty}v_{\nu}
,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\nu):=\mathcal{L}(\nu)/q^{-1}\mathcal{L}(\nu)
, and
b_{\nu}:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v_{\nu})
.

In the
n=2
case, we define a classical weight module to be a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}\mathbf{K}_{\nu}^{\oplus m_{\nu}}
for some
m_{\nu}\geq 0
. Then, each classical weight module
M
admits an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight space decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}M_{\nu}
as in the
n\geq 3
case.

5.1.
Modified action of
B_{1}

Let
M=\bigoplus_{\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}}M_{\nu}
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product.

Let
\widetilde{B}_{1}
be a linear operator on
M
defined by

\widetilde{B}_{1}v=\begin{cases}v\quad&\text{ if }v\in M_{\nu}\text{ for some }\nu>0,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }v\in M_{0},\\
-v\quad&\text{ if }v\in M_{\nu}\text{ for some }\nu<0.\end{cases}

Then,
\widetilde{B}_{1}
preserves
\mathcal{L}_{M}
. Hence, it induces a
\mathbb{C}
-linear operator
\widetilde{B}_{1}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
and consider the irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
\mathbf{K}_{\nu}=\mathbf{K}v_{\nu}
. Let
V_{\natural}=\mathbf{K}u_{1}\oplus\mathbf{K}u_{2}
denote the natural representation of
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. Recall from Proposition
4.2.3
that
\mathbf{K}_{\nu}\otimes V_{\natural}
is a classical weight module. Set

(20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&v^{\prime}_{\nu,\pm}:=v_{\nu}\otimes(u_{1}\pm q^{\pm\nu}u_{2}),\\
&v_{\nu,\pm}:=\frac{1}{\operatorname{lt}(v^{\prime}_{\nu,\pm})}v^{\prime}_{\nu,\pm}=\begin{cases}v_{\nu}\otimes(q^{\mp\nu}u_{1}\pm u_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}v_{\nu}\otimes(u_{1}\pm u_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
v_{\nu}\otimes(u_{1}\pm q^{\pm\nu}u_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu<0.\end{cases}\end{split}

By Lemma
4.3.1
, we have

(21)

\displaystyle B_{1}v_{\nu,\pm}=[\nu\pm 1]v_{\nu,\pm}.

Since
\dim\mathbf{K}_{\nu}\otimes V_{\natural}=2
, we obtain the irreducible decomposition

\mathbf{K}_{\nu}\otimes V_{\natural}=\mathbf{K}v_{\nu,+}\oplus\mathbf{K}v_{\nu,-}\simeq\mathbf{K}_{\nu+1}\oplus\mathbf{K}_{\nu-1}.

By the definition (
20
) of
v_{\nu,\pm}
, we see that

(22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(v_{\nu,\pm})=\begin{cases}\pm b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{\nu}\otimes(\overline{u}_{1}\pm\overline{u}_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu<0.\end{cases}

Proposition 5.1.1
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\begin{cases}\pm b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>1,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }\nu=\pm 1,\\
b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }|\nu|>1,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }|\nu|=1,\\
b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\end{cases}\\
\widetilde{B}_{1}(b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\begin{cases}\pm b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0,\\
b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }|\nu|>0,\\
b_{\nu}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proof.

The assertion is a consequence of formulas (
21
) and (
22
).
∎

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product.

Definition 5.1.2
.

A vector
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
is said to be
B_{1}
-homogeneous of degree
\nu
if it belongs to
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,\nu}+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M,-\nu}
for some
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
such that
\nu\geq 0
. If
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
is
B_{1}
-homogeneous of degree
\nu
, we write
\deg_{1}(b)=\nu
.

The following lemmas are clear from the definitions and previous results.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}\setminus\{0\}
be
B_{1}
-homogeneous. Then, we have exactly one of either
\widetilde{B}_{1}b=0
or
\widetilde{B}_{1}^{2}b=b
. Furthermore,
(1\pm\widetilde{B}_{1})b
is an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector of weight
\pm\deg_{1}(b)
.

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Let
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
be
B_{1}
-homogeneous. Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}\deg_{1}(b)-1\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)>0,\\
1\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)=0,\end{cases}\\
&\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\deg_{1}(b)+1,\\
&\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)>1,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)=1,\\
b\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)=0,\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)>0,\\
b\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)=0.\end{cases}\end{split}

We want to describe the action of
\widetilde{B}_{1}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
for an arbitrary
d\geq 0
. The space
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
possesses a crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}:=\{\overline{u}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\overline{u}_{i_{d}}\mid i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\in\{1,2\}\}
. For the reader’s convenience, we write down the crystal structure of
\mathcal{B}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\natural}
, which can be derived from equations (
3
) in page
3
: For each
\overline{u}=\overline{u}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\overline{u}_{i_{d}}
,
i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\in\{1,2\}
, we have

(23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{F}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})<1,\\
\widetilde{F}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq 1,\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\leq 1,\\
\widetilde{E}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>1,\end{cases}\\
&\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+1\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})<1,\\
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq 1,\end{cases}\\
&\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\begin{cases}0\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\leq 1,\\
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})-1\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>1,\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{F}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\widetilde{F}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2},\\
&\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\begin{cases}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0,\\
\widetilde{E}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>0,\end{cases}\\
&\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}),\\
&\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1.\end{split}

Proposition 5.1.5
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Let
b\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
be
B_{1}
-homogeneous and
\overline{u}=\overline{u}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\overline{u}_{i_{d}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
. Then,
b\otimes\overline{u}
is
B_{1}
-homogeneous. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\begin{cases}\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}),\\
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}},\\
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}},\end{cases}\\
&\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}\quad&\text{ if }\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}),\\
b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}\overline{u}\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}},\\
b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}\overline{u}\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
d
; the
d=0
case is clear. Let
d\geq 0
and assume that the proposition is true, and let us prove the
d+1
case. During the proof below, we often use Lemma
5.1.4
and equations (
23
) without mentioning one by one. First, we compute
\deg_{1}
and
\widetilde{B}_{1}
for
b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}
.

Suppose that
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>0
. Then, we have
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})
and
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\varepsilon(\overline{u})-1
.

•

When
\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\geq 2.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})-1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}),\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\\
&=\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}.\end{split}

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}}
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq 1.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})-1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}),\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>1,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=1\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>1,\\
0\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=1\end{cases}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}).\end{split}

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\geq 2.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})+1,\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}).\end{split}

This case-by-case analysis proves the assertion for the
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>0
case.

Next, suppose that
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0
. Then, we have
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+1
and
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=0
.

•

When
\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})>1.

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})-1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}),\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})&=\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\\
&=\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}.\end{split}

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-\deg_{1}(\overline{u})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}}
. If
\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})
, then we must have
\deg_{1}(b)=\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+1
. Hence, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})=1.

Therefore, we obtain

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-1=0=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}),

and

\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=0=b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}).

On the other hand, if
\deg_{1}(b)\leq\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})
, then we have
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}
, and hence

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1=1.

Therefore,
\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})
and
\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})
are the same as before.

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}
. In this case, we have
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}}
. Hence, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0.

Therefore, we obtain

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=1=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})+1,

and

\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1})=b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}=b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}).

This case-by-case analysis proves the assertion for the
\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0
case.

Now, let us compute
\deg_{1}
and
\widetilde{B}_{1}
for
b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}
.

•

When
\deg_{1}(b)>\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\geq 1.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})+1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\\
&=\deg_{1}(b)-\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})+\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}),\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\\
&=\widetilde{B}_{1}b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}.\end{split}

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{ev}}
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})\geq 0.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})+1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}),\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\begin{cases}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>0,\\
b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0\end{cases}\\
&=\begin{cases}b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})>0,\\
b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{1}\quad&\text{ if }\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})=0\end{cases}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}).\end{split}

•

When
\varphi_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})-\deg_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0,\mathrm{odd}}
. In this case, we have

\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+1\geq 1.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\deg_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})+1\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u})+2\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})+1,\end{split}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2})&=\widetilde{B}_{1}(b\otimes\overline{u})\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}\\
&=b\otimes\widetilde{F}_{1}(\overline{u}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}).\end{split}

Thus, the proof completes.
∎

Corollary 5.1.6
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module with a crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
. For each
b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
, the following hold:

(1)

b
is
B_{1}
-homogeneous;

\deg_{1}(b)=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{1}(b)\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\varepsilon_{1}(b)+1\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

(2)

\widetilde{B}_{1}b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}\sqcup\{0\}
;

\widetilde{B}_{1}b=\begin{cases}\widetilde{E}_{1}b\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
\widetilde{F}_{1}b\quad&\text{ if }\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{B}_{M}
can be embedded into
\mathcal{B}_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
for some
d\geq 0
, it suffices to prove the assertions for
M=V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
. The latter follows from Proposition
5.1.5
by considering
\mathbf{K}_{0}\otimes V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}\simeq V_{\natural}^{\otimes d}
.
∎

Corollary 5.1.7
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbf{U}
-module with a crystal basis
\mathcal{B}_{M}
. Then, the following forms an orthonormal basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{M}
consisting of
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vectors:

\{b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}\mid\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}\and\varepsilon_{1}(b)=0\}\sqcup\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(b\pm\widetilde{F}_{1}(b))\mid b\in\mathcal{B}_{M}\and\varphi_{1}(b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}\}.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Lemma
5.1.3
and Corollary
5.1.6
.
∎

Remark 5.1.8
.

In the general
n\geq 3
case, we define
\widetilde{B}_{i}
and
\deg_{i}
for all
i\in I
in the obvious way.

5.2.
Based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structures of irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-modules

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and consider the irreducible
\mathbf{U}
-module
V(\lambda)
. Since the weights of
V(\lambda)
belong to the interval
[-\lambda,\lambda]_{p(\lambda)}
, each vector in
V(\lambda)
is an
X^{\imath}
-weight vector of weight
\overline{\lambda}
.

Recall that the set
\{F_{1}^{(k)}v_{\lambda}\mid k\in[0,\lambda]\}
forms the canonical basis
G(\lambda)
, and the set
\{B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(k)}v_{\lambda}\mid k\in[0,\lambda]\}
forms the
\imath
canonical basis
G^{\imath}(\lambda)
of
V(\lambda)
. Viewing
V(\lambda)
as a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module in the sense of Example
3.4.5
, we have
G^{\imath}(\widetilde{F}_{1}^{k}b_{\lambda})=B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(k)}v_{\lambda}
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

V(\lambda)\{\geq m\}:=\mathbf{K}\{B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(k)}v_{\lambda}\mid k\geq m\}.

Then, we have a filtration

0=V(\lambda)\{\geq\lambda+1\}\subseteq V(\lambda)\{\geq\lambda-1\}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V(\lambda)\{\geq\lambda-2\lfloor\tfrac{\lambda}{2}\rfloor-1\}=V(\lambda)

of
X^{\imath}
-weight modules, where
\lfloor r\rfloor
denotes the greatest integer not greater than
r\in\mathbb{Q}
. In fact, by Proposition
4.4.1
, we have

B_{1}B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(\lambda-2m-1)}v_{\lambda}\equiv[\lambda-2m]B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(\lambda-2m)}v_{\lambda},\quad B_{1}B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(\lambda-2m)}v_{\lambda}\equiv[\lambda-2m]B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(\lambda-2m-1)}v_{\lambda}

modulo
V(\lambda)\{\geq\lambda-2m+1\}
.

For each
\nu\in[-\lambda,\lambda]_{p(\lambda)}
, set

b_{\lambda,\nu}:=\begin{cases}b_{\lambda}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\widetilde{F}_{1}^{|\nu|-1}b_{\lambda}\pm\widetilde{F}_{1}^{|\nu|}b_{\lambda})\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0.\end{cases}

Then, by Corollary
5.1.7
, the vector
b_{\lambda,\nu}\in\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda)
is an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector of weight
\nu
, and the set
\{b_{\lambda,\nu}\mid\nu\in[-\lambda,\lambda]_{p(\lambda)}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\lambda)
.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Let
\lambda\in X^{+}
, and
\nu\in[-\lambda,\lambda]_{p(\lambda)}
. Then,
G^{\imath}(b_{\lambda,\nu})
is a sum of
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vectors of weights in
\{\nu,\pm(|\nu|+2),\pm(|\nu|+4),\ldots,\pm\lambda\}
.

Proof.

For each
\nu\in[-\lambda,\lambda]_{p(\lambda)}
, define
B_{1,\nu}\in\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
by

B_{1,\nu}:=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(|\nu|-1)}\pm B_{1,p(\lambda)}^{(|\nu|)})\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0.\end{cases}

Then, by above, we have

B_{1,\nu}v_{\lambda}=G^{\imath}(b_{\lambda,\nu}),

and

B_{1}B_{1,\nu}v_{\lambda}\equiv[\nu]B_{1,\nu}v_{\lambda}\quad\pmod{V(\lambda)\{\geq|\nu|+1\}}.

Then, the assertion follows by descending induction on
|\nu|
.
∎

6.
n=3
case

In this section, we consider the
n=3
case. In this case, we can identify
X=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
X^{+}=\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{\geq 0}
,
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}=\mathbb{Z}
,
X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
X^{\imath}=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{2}
.

6.1.
Lowering and raising operators

Set

X_{2}=B_{2,+}\{l_{1};0\},\quad Y_{2}=B_{2,-}\{l_{1};0\},\quad l:=l_{1}.

In this section, when there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate
X_{2}
and
Y_{2}
as
X
and
Y
, respectively.

By Lemma
4.2.7
and equation (
12
), we have

(Xu,v)=(u,Y\frac{\{l;-1\}}{\{l;0\}}v),\quad(Yu,v)=(u,X\frac{\{l;1\}}{\{l;0\}}v).

for all
u,v\in M
with
M
being a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k}}^{+}
, and consider the corresponding irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module
V(\nu)
.

Lemma 6.1.1
.

Let
k\geq 0
. Then, we have

XY^{(k)}v_{\nu}=[2\nu-k+1]Y^{(k-1)}v_{\nu},

where
Y^{(k)}:=\frac{1}{[k]!}Y^{k}
.

Proof.

By equation (
13
), we have

[X,Y]=[l^{2};0].

Then, the assertion is verified by induction on
k
.
∎

From this lemma (with a standard argument), we see that
\{Y^{(k)}v_{\nu}\mid k\in[0,2\nu]\}
forms a basis of
V(\nu)
. Note that
Y^{(k)}v_{\nu}\in V(\nu)_{\nu-k}
. For each
k\in[0,2\nu]
, we can choose
c_{k,\nu}\in\mathbf{K}^{\times}
in a way such that
\operatorname{lt}(c_{k,\nu}Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})=1
. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu}:=\begin{cases}c_{k,\nu}Y^{(k)}v_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }k\in[0,2\nu],\\
0\quad&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then,
\{\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu}\mid k\in[0,2\nu]\}
forms an almost orthonormal basis of
V(\nu)
.

Using Lemma
6.1.1
, we see by induction on
k
that

\displaystyle(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu},Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})_{\nu}={2\nu\brack k}\frac{\{\nu\}}{\{\nu-k\}}.

This shows that for each
k\in[0,2\nu]
, we have

(24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})=\frac{1}{2}((2\nu-k)k+\nu-|\nu-k|)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}(2\nu-k+1)k\quad&\text{ if }k\leq\nu,\\
\frac{1}{2}(2\nu-k)(k+1)\quad&\text{ if }k\geq\nu,\end{cases}\\
&\operatorname{lc}(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\quad&\text{ if }k=\nu>0,\\
1\quad&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{split}

Consequently, we obtain

\operatorname{lt}(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
q^{\frac{1}{2}(2\nu-k+1)n}\quad&\text{ if }0\leq k<\nu,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}\nu(\nu+1)}\quad&\text{ if }k=\nu>0,\\
q^{\frac{1}{2}(2\nu-k)(k+1)}\quad&\text{ if }\nu<k\leq 2\nu.\end{cases}

Remark 6.1.2
.

For each
k\in[0,\nu]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg(\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\{\nu-j\})=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(\nu-j)=\frac{1}{2}(2\nu-k+1)k,\\
&\operatorname{lc}(\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\{\nu-j\})=1.\end{split}

Hence, we may choose

(25)

\displaystyle c_{k,\nu}:=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\{\nu-j\}}\quad&\text{ if }0\leq k<\nu,\\
\sqrt{2}\prod_{j=0}^{\nu-1}\frac{1}{\{\nu-j\}}\quad&\text{ if }k=\nu>0,\\
\prod_{j=0}^{2\nu-k-1}\frac{1}{\{\nu-j\}}\quad&\text{ if }\nu<k\leq 2\nu.\end{cases}

In the sequel,
c_{k,\nu}
always means this value. Note that we have

\psi^{\imath}_{\nu}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu})=\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu}

for all
k\in[0,2\nu]
, and

c_{0,\nu}=c_{2\nu,\nu}=1.

Definition 6.1.3
.

The lowering operator
\widetilde{Y}
and the raising operator
\widetilde{X}
are
\mathbf{K}
-linear endomorphisms on
V(\nu)
defined by

\widetilde{Y}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu}):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{Y}^{k+1}v_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }k\in[0,2\nu],\\
0\quad&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}\quad\widetilde{X}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu}):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }k\in[0,2\nu],\\
0\quad&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Clearly, the operators
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{X}
preserve
\mathcal{L}(\nu)
. Hence, they induce linear operators
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{X}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\nu)
.

Since each finite-dimensional classical weight module is completely reducible, we can extend the definitions of
\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{X}
on the irreducible modules to the finite-dimensional classical weight modules. The following are counterparts of well-known results about the Kashiwara operators. The proofs are similar, so we omit them.

Proposition 6.1.4
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module. Let
\xi\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
. Then, we have
YM_{\xi}=\widetilde{Y}M_{\xi}
.

Proposition 6.1.5
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product. Then, for each
u,v\in\mathcal{L}_{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\widetilde{Y}u,v)-(u,\widetilde{X}v)\in q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}.\end{split}

6.2.
Tensor product rule

In this subsection, we investigate the behavior of
\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{X}
on tensor product modules. Let
V_{\natural}=\mathbf{K}u_{1}\oplus\mathbf{K}u_{2}\oplus\mathbf{K}u_{3}
denote the natural representation of
\mathbf{U}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{3})
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module equipped with a contragredient Hermitian inner product, and
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
. For
v\in M_{\nu}
, set

v^{\prime}_{\pm}:=v\otimes(u_{1}\pm q^{\pm\nu}u_{2}),\quad v_{0}:=v\otimes u_{3},

and

v_{\pm}:=\frac{\operatorname{lt}(v)}{\operatorname{lt}(v^{\prime}_{\pm})}v^{\prime}_{\pm}=\begin{cases}q^{\mp\nu}v^{\prime}_{\pm}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu>0,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}v^{\prime}_{\pm}\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0,\\
v^{\prime}_{\pm}\quad&\text{ if }\pm\nu<0.\end{cases}

Then, we have

(26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg(v^{\prime}_{\pm})=\deg(v)+\max\{\pm\nu,0\},\quad\deg(v_{0})=\deg(v),\\
&\operatorname{lc}(v^{\prime}_{\pm})=\begin{cases}\operatorname{lc}(v)\quad&\text{ if }\nu\neq 0,\\
\sqrt{2}\operatorname{lc}(v)\quad&\text{ if }\nu=0.\end{cases}\end{split}

Lemma 6.2.1
.

We have
v^{\prime}_{\pm}\in(M\otimes V_{\natural})_{\nu\pm 1}
and
v_{0}\in(M\otimes V_{\natural})_{\nu}
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Lemma
4.3.1
.
∎

For convenience, we write down easy formulas:

(27)

\displaystyle v\otimes u_{1}=\frac{1}{\{\nu\}}(q^{-\nu}v^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}v^{\prime}_{-}),

(28)

\displaystyle v\otimes u_{2}=\frac{1}{\{\nu\}}(v^{\prime}_{+}-v^{\prime}_{-}).

Proposition 6.2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Xv^{\prime}_{+}=q^{-1}(Xv)^{\prime}_{+},\\
&Yv^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu\}}(Yv)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}\{\nu+1\}v_{0}+\frac{q^{\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu\}}(Xv)^{\prime}_{-},\\
&Xv^{\prime}_{-}=\frac{q^{-\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu\}}(Yv)^{\prime}_{+}-q^{-\nu}\{\nu-1\}v_{0}+\frac{\{\nu-1\}}{\{\nu\}}(Xv)^{\prime}_{-},\\
&Yv^{\prime}_{-}=q^{-1}(Yv)^{\prime}_{-},\\
&Xv_{0}=v^{\prime}_{+}+q(Xv)_{0},\\
&Yv_{0}=q(Yv)_{0}-v^{\prime}_{-}.\\
\end{split}

Proof.

Since
\Delta(B_{2})=B_{2}\otimes K_{2}^{-1}+1\otimes B_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{2}v^{\prime}_{\pm}\\
&=B_{2}v\otimes(u_{1}\pm q^{\pm\nu-1}u_{2})\pm q^{\pm\nu}v\otimes u_{3}\\
&=(B_{2,+}+B_{2,-})v\otimes u_{1}\pm q^{\pm\nu-1}(B_{2,+}+B_{2,-})v\otimes u_{2}\pm q^{\pm\nu}v_{0}\\
&=\frac{1}{\{\nu+1\}}(q^{-\nu-1}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu+1}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-})+\frac{1}{\{\nu-1\}}(q^{-\nu+1}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu-1}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{-})\\
&\quad\pm q^{\pm\nu-1}(\frac{1}{\{\nu+1\}}((B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{+}-(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-})+\frac{1}{\{\nu-1\}}((B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}-(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{-}))\pm q^{\pm\nu}v_{0}.\end{split}

For the last equality, we used equations (
27
) and (
28
). Rearranging this, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{2}v^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{q^{-1}\{\nu\}}{\{\nu+1\}}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{+}+\frac{q^{\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu+1\}}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-}+(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}v_{0},\\
&B_{2}v^{\prime}_{-}=(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-}+\frac{q^{-\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu-1\}}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}+\frac{q^{-1}\{\nu\}}{\{\nu-1\}}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{-}-q^{-\nu}v_{0}.\end{split}

By weight consideration, we conclude

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{2,+}v^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{q^{-1}\{\nu\}}{\{\nu+1\}}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{+},\\
&B_{2,-}v^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{q^{\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu+1\}}(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-}+(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}v_{0},\\
&B_{2,+}v^{\prime}_{-}=(B_{2,+}v)^{\prime}_{-}+\frac{q^{-\nu}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu-1\}}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{+}-q^{-\nu}v_{0},\\
&B_{2,-}v^{\prime}_{-}=\frac{q^{-1}\{\nu\}}{\{\nu-1\}}(B_{2,-}v)^{\prime}_{-}.\end{split}

These imply the first four assertions.

Also, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{2}v_{0}&=v\otimes u_{2}+qB_{2}v\otimes u_{3}\\
&=\frac{1}{\{\nu\}}(v^{\prime}_{+}-v^{\prime}_{-})+q(B_{2,+}v)_{0}+q(B_{2,-}v)_{0}.\end{split}

Again, we used equation (
28
) for the last equality. By weight consideration, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{2,+}v_{0}=\frac{1}{\{\nu\}}v^{\prime}_{+}+q(B_{2,+}v)_{0},\\
&B_{2,-}v_{0}=-\frac{1}{\{\nu\}}v^{\prime}_{-}+q(B_{2,-}v)_{0}.\end{split}

These imply the remaining assertions. Thus, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.2.3
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional classical weight module,
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}
, and
v\in M_{\nu}
. Then, for each
k\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}Y^{(k)}v^{\prime}_{+}&=\frac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k)}v)^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}\{\nu+1\}(Y^{(k-1)}v)_{0}\\
&\quad-\frac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k-2)}v)^{\prime}_{-}+\frac{q^{\nu-k+1}(q-q^{-1})}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k-1)}Xv)^{\prime}_{-},\\
Y^{(k)}v_{0}&=q^{k}(Y^{(k)}v)_{0}-(Y^{(k-1)}v)^{\prime}_{-},\\
Y^{(k)}v^{\prime}_{-}&=q^{-k}(Y^{(k)}v)^{\prime}_{-}.\end{split}

Proof.

Using Proposition
6.2.2
, the assertion is verified by induction on
k
.
∎

Proposition 6.2.4
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. Then, for each
k\in[0,2\nu+2]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&X(v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}=0,\\
&Y^{(k)}(v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}=\frac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}+q^{\nu}\{\nu+1\}(Y^{(k-1)}v_{\nu})_{0}-\frac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k-2)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{-},\\
&\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}(v_{\nu})_{+})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((v_{\nu})_{+})\quad&\text{ if }k=0,\\
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu})_{0})\quad&\text{ if }1\leq k\leq 2\nu+1,\\
-\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu}v_{\nu})_{-})\quad&\text{ if }k=2\nu+2.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proof.

The first and second assertions are verified by using Proposition
6.2.2
and Lemma
6.2.3
. Let us prove the third assertion. By the second equality and the definition of
\widetilde{Y}
, we can write

(29)

\displaystyle d_{0}\widetilde{Y}^{k}(v_{\nu})_{+}=d_{1}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu})_{+}+d_{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu})_{0}-d_{3}(\widetilde{Y}^{k-2}v_{\nu})_{-},

where
d_{0},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}\in\mathbf{K}
such that
\operatorname{lc}(d_{j})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\deg(d_{0})=\deg(Y^{(k)}(v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}),\quad\deg(d_{1})=\deg(\tfrac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}),\\
&\deg(d_{2})=\deg(q^{\nu}\{\nu+1\}(Y^{(k-1)}v_{\nu})_{0}),\quad\deg(d_{3})=\deg(\tfrac{\{\nu+1\}}{\{\nu-k+1\}}(Y^{(k-2)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{-}).\end{split}

More explicitly, by equations (
24
) and (
26
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg(d_{1})&=\nu+1-|\nu-k+1|+\frac{1}{2}(n(2\nu-k)+\nu-|\nu-k|)+\max\{\nu-k,0\}\\
&=\nu+1+\frac{1}{2}(k+1)(2\nu-k)-|\nu-k+1|,\\
\deg(d_{2})&=2\nu+1+\frac{1}{2}((k-1)(2\nu-k+1)+\nu-|\nu-k+1|),\\
\deg(d_{3})&=\nu+1-|\nu-k+1|+\frac{1}{2}((k-2)(2\nu-k+2)+\nu-|\nu-k+2|)\\
&\quad+\max\{-\nu+k-2,0\}\\
&=\nu+1+\frac{1}{2}(k-2)(2\nu-k+3)-|\nu-k+1|.\end{split}

Here, we used an easy formula

\max\{x,0\}=\frac{1}{2}(x+|x|).

Then, we compute as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\deg(d_{2})-\deg(d_{1})&=\nu+\frac{1}{2}(-3\nu+3k-1+|\nu-k+1|)\\
&=\begin{cases}k\quad&\text{ if }0\leq k\leq\nu+1,\\
-\nu+2k-1\quad&\text{ if }\nu+1\leq k\leq 2\nu+2,\end{cases}\\
\deg(d_{2})-\deg(d_{3})&=\nu+\frac{1}{2}(3\nu-3k+5+|\nu-k+1|)\\
&=\begin{cases}3\nu-2k+3\quad&\text{ if }k\leq\nu+1,\\
2\nu-k+2\quad&\text{ if }\nu+1\leq k\leq 2\nu+2.\end{cases}\end{split}

From these, we obtain

(30)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\deg(d_{3})\underset{3(\nu-k+1)}{\leq}\deg(d_{1})\underset{k}{\leq}\deg(d_{2})\quad&\text{ if }0\leq k\leq\nu+1,\\
\deg(d_{1})\underset{-3(\nu-k+1)}{\leq}\deg(d_{3})\underset{2\nu-k+2}{\leq}\deg(d_{2})\quad&\text{ if }\nu+1\leq k\leq 2\nu+2.\end{cases}

Here,
a\underset{c}{\leq}b
means
c=b-a\geq 0
.

On the other hand, since the terms in the right-hand side of (
29
) are orthogonal to each other, we have

\deg(d_{0})=\begin{cases}\deg(d_{1})\quad&\text{ if }k=0,\\
\max\{\deg(d_{1}),\deg(d_{2})\}\quad&\text{ if }k=1,\\
\max\{\deg(d_{1}),\deg(d_{2}),\deg(d_{3})\}\quad&\text{ if }2\leq k\leq 2\nu,\\
\max\{\deg(d_{2}),\deg(d_{3})\}\quad&\text{ if }k=2\nu+1,\\
\deg(d_{3})\quad&\text{ if }k=2\nu+2.\end{cases}

Combining this and inequalities (
30
), we obtain

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\widetilde{Y}^{n}(v_{\nu})_{+})=\begin{cases}\frac{\operatorname{lc}(d_{1})}{\operatorname{lc}(d_{0})}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((v_{\nu})_{+})\quad&\text{ if }k=0,\\
\frac{\operatorname{lc}(d_{2})}{\operatorname{lc}(d_{0})}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu})_{0})\quad&\text{ if }1\leq k\leq 2\nu+1,\\
-\frac{\operatorname{lc}(d_{3})}{\operatorname{lc}(d_{0})}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu}v_{\nu})_{-})\quad&\text{ if }k=2\nu+2.\end{cases}

Noting that
\operatorname{lc}(d_{j})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for all
j=0,1,2,3
, and that

\operatorname{lc}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}(v_{\nu})_{+})=\operatorname{lc}((v_{\nu})_{+})=\operatorname{lc}((\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu})_{0})=\operatorname{lc}((\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu}v_{\nu})_{-})=1,

we finally obtain the required equation.
∎

The following two results can be proved in a similar way to Proposition
6.2.4
.

Proposition 6.2.5
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
be such that
\nu>0
. Set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&v^{\prime}:=(Yv_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}-q^{-1}[2\nu](v_{\nu})_{0},\\
&v:=\frac{1}{\operatorname{lt}(v^{\prime})}v^{\prime}.\end{split}

Then, for each
k\in[0,2\nu]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Xv^{\prime}=0,\\
&Y^{(k)}v^{\prime}=\frac{\{\nu\}}{\{\nu-k\}}([k+1](Y^{(k+1)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}-q^{-1}[2\nu-2k](Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})_{0}+[2\nu-k+1](Y^{(k-1)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{-}),\\
&\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k+1}v_{\nu})_{+})\quad&\text{ if }0\leq k<\nu,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{\nu+1}v_{\nu})_{+}+(\widetilde{Y}^{\nu-1}v_{\nu})_{-})\quad&\text{ if }k=\nu,\\
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}v_{\nu})_{-})\quad&\text{ if }\nu<k\leq 2\nu.\end{cases}\end{split}

Proposition 6.2.6
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
be such that
\nu>0
. Set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&v^{\prime}:=-[2](Y^{(2)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}+q^{-\nu-1}[2\nu-1]\{\nu-1\}(Yv_{\nu})_{0}+[2\nu][2\nu-1](v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{-},\\
&v:=\frac{1}{\operatorname{lt}(v^{\prime})}v^{\prime}.\end{split}

Then, for each
k\in[0,2\nu-2]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Xv^{\prime}=0,\\
&Y^{(k)}v^{\prime}=-\frac{[k+2][k+1]\{\nu-1\}}{\{\nu-k-1\}}(Y^{(k+2)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{+}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt+q^{-\nu-1}[k+1][2\nu-k-1]\{\nu-1\}(Y^{(k+1)}v_{\nu})_{0}+\frac{[2\nu-k][2\nu-k-1]\{\nu-1\}}{\{\nu-k-1\}}(Y^{(k)}v_{\nu})^{\prime}_{-}),\\
&\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}v)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k}v_{\nu})_{-})\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{ if }0\leq k<\nu-1,\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{\nu+1}v_{\nu})_{+}-(\widetilde{Y}^{\nu-1}v_{\nu})_{-})\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{ if }k=\nu-1,\\
-\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}((\widetilde{Y}^{k+2}v_{\nu})_{+})\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{ if }\nu-1<k\leq 2\nu-2.\end{cases}\end{split}

6.3.
Based module structures of irreducible
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules

In this subsection, we study
V(\nu)
,
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
, and show that it admits a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module structure.

Proposition 6.3.1
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. Then,
V(\nu)
is a standard
X^{\imath}
-weight module. Furthermore, the highest weight vector
v_{\nu}
has the following
X^{\imath}
-weight vector decomposition

\displaystyle\begin{split}v_{\nu}&=\frac{1}{2}(v_{\nu}+(-1)^{\nu}Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})+\frac{1}{2}(v_{\nu}-(-1)^{\nu}Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})\in V(\nu)_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{0})}\oplus V(\nu)_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{1})}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})+\frac{1}{2}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})\in V(\nu)_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu})}\oplus V(\nu)_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu-1})}\end{split}

Proof.

Let
M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes\nu}
. Then, its canonical
X^{\imath}
-weight module structure is standard by Proposition
4.4.7
. Define
u^{\pm\nu}\in M
inductively by

u^{\pm 1}:=u_{1}\pm u_{2},\quad u^{\pm k}:=(u^{\pm(k-1)})_{\pm}=u^{\pm(k-1)}\otimes(q^{-k+1}u_{1}\pm u_{2}).

Explicitly, we have

u^{\pm\nu}=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{\nu}\in\{1,2\}}(\pm 1)^{\sharp\{k\mid i_{k}=2\}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\\
i_{k}=1\end{subarray}}q^{-k+1}u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{\nu}}.

Hence, we obtain

u^{\nu}+u^{-\nu}=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{\nu}\in\{1,2\}\\
\sharp\{k\mid i_{k}=2\}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\\
i_{k}=1\end{subarray}}q^{-k+1}u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{\nu}}\in M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{0})},

and

u^{\nu}-u^{-\nu}=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{\nu}\in\{1,2\}\\
\sharp\{k\mid i_{k}=2\}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\\
i_{k}=1\end{subarray}}q^{-k+1}u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{\nu}}\in M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{1})}.

These imply that

u^{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}(u^{\nu}+u^{-\nu})+\frac{1}{2}(u^{\nu}-u^{-\nu})\in M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{0})}\oplus M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{1})}

is the
X^{\imath}
-weight vector decomposition of
u^{\nu}
.

On the other hand, by the definition of
u^{\pm\nu}
and Proposition
6.2.4
, we have

Xu^{\nu}=0,\quad lu^{\nu}=q^{\nu}u^{\nu},\quad Y^{(2\nu)}u^{\nu}=(-1)^{\nu}u^{-\nu}.

Therefore, the
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-submodule
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu}
generated by
u^{\nu}
is isomorphic to
V(\nu)
, and it is also generated by two
X^{\imath}
-weight vectors
u^{\nu}+u^{-\nu}
and
u^{\nu}-u^{-\nu}
. Hence,
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu}
is a standard
X^{\imath}
-weight module by Propositions
3.3.8
, and
4.4.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

Now, we set

V(\nu)_{\mathbf{A}}:=\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}v_{\nu},

and call it the
\mathbf{A}
-form of
V(\nu)
. That it is actually an
\mathbf{A}
-form of
V(\nu)
in the sense of Definition
3.4.1
will be proved later.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
,
M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes\nu}
. Let
u^{\nu}\in M
be as before. Then, we have

\psi^{\imath}_{M}(u^{\nu})=u^{\nu}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3.2
, we see that
M
has no
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vectors of weight greater than
\nu
or less than
-\nu
.

The vector
u^{\nu}
, expanded by the basis
\{u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{\nu}}\mid i_{1},\ldots,i_{\nu}\in\{1,2,3\}\}
, contains
u_{2}^{\otimes\nu}
with coefficient
1
. Note that we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{E}_{1}(\overline{u}_{1}^{\otimes\nu})=0,\\
&\widetilde{F}_{1}^{\nu-1}(\overline{u}_{1}^{\otimes\nu})=\overline{u}_{1}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu-1},\\
&\widetilde{F}_{1}^{\nu}(\overline{u}_{1}^{\otimes\nu})=\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu}.\end{split}

This together with Proposition
5.2.1
, implies that
G^{\imath}(\overline{u}_{1}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu-1}+\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu})
is an
X_{\mathfrak{k}}
-weight vector of weight
\nu
. Furthermore, the vector
G^{\imath}(\overline{u}_{1}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu-1}+\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu})
, expanded by the basis
\{u_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes u_{i_{\nu}}\mid i_{1},\ldots,i_{\nu}\in\{1,2,3\}\}
, contains
u_{2}^{\otimes\nu}
with coefficient
1
. Since
\dim M_{\nu}=1
by Proposition
4.3.2
, we conclude that

u^{\nu}=G^{\imath}(\overline{u}_{1}\otimes\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu-1}+\overline{u}_{2}^{\otimes\nu}),

and hence, it is
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
-invariant. This completes the proof
∎

Proposition 6.3.3
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
.

(1)

For each
k\geq 0
, we have

B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}),\ B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})\in V(\nu)_{\mathbf{A}}.

(2)

We have
B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=0
for all
k>\nu
.

(3)

We have
B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=0
for all
k>\nu-1
.

(4)

\{B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})\mid k\in[0,\nu]\}\sqcup\{B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k-1)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})\mid k\in[0,\nu-1]\}
forms a free
\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
-basis of
\mathcal{L}(\nu)
.

(5)

\{\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))\mid k\in[0,\nu]\}\sqcup\{\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k-1)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))\mid k\in[0,\nu-1]\}
forms a
\mathbb{C}
-basis of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\nu)
.

(6)

We have
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu}
for all
k\in[0,\nu]
.

(7)

We have
\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu}
for all
k\in[0,\nu-1]
.

Proof.

Let
M=V_{\natural}^{\otimes\nu}
, and
u^{\nu}\in M
be as before. Then, there exists a
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module homomorphism
\phi:V(\nu)\rightarrow M
such that
\phi(v_{\nu})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}u^{\nu}
. We identify
V(\nu)
with
\phi(V(\nu))
. By Proposition
6.3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=B_{2,(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu})}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=2B_{2,(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu})}^{(k)}v_{\nu}\in V(\nu)_{\mathbf{A}},\\
&B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=B_{2,(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu-1})}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=2B_{2,(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu-1})}^{(k)}v_{\nu}\in V(\nu)_{\mathbf{A}}.\end{split}

This proves assertion (
1
).

Exchanging the roles of
B_{1}
with
B_{2}
in Proposition
4.3.2
, we see that the
B_{2}
-eigenvalues of
M
are
[a]
with
a\in[-\nu,\nu]
. Since
v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}\in M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu})}
, by Lemma
4.4.6
, it is a sum of
B_{2}
-eigenvectors of eigenvalues
[a]
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p(\nu)}
. Therefore, we see that

B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=0\quad\text{ if }k>\nu.

Similarly, since
v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}\in M_{(\overline{\nu},\overline{\nu-1})}
, it is a sum of
B_{2}
-eigenvectors of eigenvalues
[a]
with
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{q(\nu)}
. Therefore, we see that

B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})=0\quad\text{ if }k>\nu-1.

These prove assertions (
2
) and (
3
).

For each
p\in\{\mathrm{ev},\mathrm{odd}\}
and
k\in[0,\nu]
, we show by induction on
k
that there exist
d_{m,k,p}\in\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
such that
d_{0,k,p}=1
if
k<\nu
,
d_{0,\nu,p}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, and
d_{m,k,p}\in q^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
if
m>0
, and

B_{2,p}^{(k)}v_{\nu}=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}d_{m,k,p}\widetilde{Y}^{k-2m}v_{\nu}.

Let
k\in[0,\nu-1]
. Then, inductively, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{2}B_{2,p}^{(k)}v_{\nu}&=(Y+X)\frac{1}{\{l;0\}}\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}d_{m,k,p}c_{k-2m,\nu}Y^{(k-2m)}v_{\nu}\\
&=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}\frac{d_{m,k,p}c_{k-2m,\nu}}{\{\nu-k+2m\}}([k-2m+1]Y^{(k-2m+1)}+[2\nu-k+2m+1]Y^{(k-2m-1)})v_{\nu}\\
&=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}\frac{d_{m,k,p}c_{k-2m,\nu}}{\{\nu-k+2m\}}(\frac{[k-2m+1]}{c_{k-2m+1,\nu}}\widetilde{Y}^{k-2m+1}+\frac{[2\nu-k+2m+1]}{c_{k-2m-1,\nu}}\widetilde{Y}^{k-2m-1})v_{\nu}.\end{split}

By degree consideration, the right-hand side is a sum of
\widetilde{Y}^{k-2m+1}
with
m\in[0,\lfloor\frac{k+1}{2}\rfloor]
whose coefficient equals
\frac{[k+1]}{\sqrt{2}^{\delta_{k,\nu-1}}}
if
m=0
, and belongs to
q^{k-1}\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
if
m\neq 0
. Then, our claim follows from the recursive formula for the
\imath
divided powers in Proposition
4.4.1
. Similarly, for each
k\in[0,\nu]
we have

B_{2,p}^{(k)}Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}=\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}d_{m,k,p}\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k+2m}v_{\nu}

for the same
d_{m,k,p}\in\mathbf{K}_{\infty}
as before. Therefore, we obtain

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu}

for each
k\in[0,\nu]
, and

\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu}

for each
k\in[0,\nu-1]
. These imply the remaining assertions.
∎

Theorem 6.3.4
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
. Then,
V(\nu)
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module. Furthermore, for each
k\in[0,\nu]
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&G^{\imath}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu})=B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}),\\
&G^{\imath}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}b_{\nu}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b_{\nu})=B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}).\end{split}

Proof.

Let
u^{\nu}\in M:=V_{\natural}^{\otimes\nu}
be as before. Then, there exists an almost isometry

\phi:V(\nu)\rightarrow\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}u^{\nu}(=\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu})

of
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-modules such that
\phi(v_{\nu})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}u^{\nu}
. Note that
u^{\nu}
belongs to the
\mathbf{A}
-form
M_{\mathbf{A}}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{A}u_{i}
. Also, by Lemma
6.3.2
, it is
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
-invariant. Then, by Propositions
4.4.5
and
6.3.3
(
1
), we have

\phi(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})),\ \phi(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))\in\dot{\mathbf{U}}^{\imath}_{\mathbf{A}}u^{\nu}\subseteq M_{\mathbf{A}},

for all
k\geq 0
, and they are
\psi^{\imath}_{M}
-invariant. Furthermore, by Proposition
6.3.3
(
6
) and (
7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\phi(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b\quad\text{ if }k\in[0,\nu],\\
&\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\phi(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu})))=\widetilde{Y}^{k}b-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b\quad\text{ if }k\in[0,\nu-1],\end{split}

where
b:=\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\phi(v_{\nu}))
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.4.6
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phi(B_{2,p(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}+Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=G^{\imath}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}b+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b)\quad\text{ if }k\in[0,\nu],\\
&\phi(B_{2,q(\nu)}^{(k)}(v_{\nu}-Y^{(2\nu)}v_{\nu}))=G^{\imath}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}b-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k}b)\quad\text{ if }k\in[0,\nu-1].\end{split}

These show that
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu}
is spanned by
G^{\imath}(\mathrm{ev}_{\infty}(\phi(\mathcal{L}(\nu))))
. Hence, by Proposition
3.4.9
(
1
),
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu}
is a based submodule of
M
. This shows that
V(\nu)=\phi^{-1}(\mathbf{U}^{\imath}u^{\nu})
is a based
\mathbf{U}^{\imath}
-module. Thus, the proof completes.
∎

Corollary 6.3.5
.

Let
\nu\in X_{\mathfrak{k},\operatorname{int}}^{+}
.

(1)

For each
k\in[0,\nu]
, we have

\widetilde{B}_{1}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu}=(\widetilde{Y}^{k}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu},

and for each
k\in[0,\nu-1]
,

\widetilde{B}_{1}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu}=(\widetilde{Y}^{k}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu}.

(2)

For each
k\in[0,\nu]
, we have

\widetilde{B}_{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu}=\begin{cases}(\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k+1})b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}}\setminus\{0\},\\
\sqrt{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{\nu-1}+\widetilde{Y}^{\nu+1})b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k=0,\\
(\widetilde{Y}^{k+1}+\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k-1})b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}}\setminus\{1\},\\
\sqrt{2}\widetilde{Y}^{\nu}b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k=1,\end{cases}

and for each
k\in[0,\nu-1]
,

\widetilde{B}_{2}(\widetilde{Y}^{k}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k})b_{\nu}=\begin{cases}(\widetilde{Y}^{k+1}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k-1})b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{ev}},\\
(\widetilde{Y}^{k-1}-\widetilde{Y}^{2\nu-k+1})b_{\nu}\quad&\text{ if }\nu-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\mathrm{odd}},\end{cases}

Example 6.3.6
.

We give graphical descriptions of
\widetilde{B}_{1}
and
\widetilde{B}_{2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{L}}(\nu)
for
\nu=2
(left) and
\nu=3
(right).
